arXiv:2608.12291v1 \[math.PR\] 12 Aug 2026

# When should one stop the most exciting game?   Sequential Inference for win-martingalesThanks: SC gratefully acknowledges support from an NSERC Postdoctoral Fellowship (PDF‑599675-2025) and a Columbia University CDFT Research Grant.

Steven Campbella and Karl Kristian EngelundbAddress: aDept. of Statistics, Columbia University, New York, NY, USA.
Address: bDept. of Mathematical Sciences, University of Copenhagen, Copenhagen, Denmark.
Email address: [kken@math.ku.dk](mailto:kken@math.ku.dk)

Date: August 24, 2026

###### Abstract.

Prediction markets have become a prominent way of aggregating beliefs about binary future events, and their price processes are often interpreted as evolving win probabilities, or “win-martingales.” Motivated by this perspective and recent work on Aldous’ “most exciting game,” we study when a decision maker should stop observing a win-martingale and make a decision about the outcome. In particular, we allow the true outcome to be revealed at a fixed finite horizon, as in a sports game or election. Under a general terminal loss and running cost, we reduce the Bayes risk to an optimal stopping problem for the win probability process. When the win-martingale is a diffusion and its volatility separates into a deterministic time factor and a state-dependent factor, a deterministic time change transforms the problem into one for a time-homogeneous diffusion with a generally time-inhomogeneous running cost.

Under explicit structural assumptions, we obtain a complete free-boundary characterization of the solution to the stopping problem in both finite and infinite horizons without discounting. We prove smooth-fit and C1C^{1} regularity of the value function, C1C^{1} regularity of the optimal stopping boundaries before the horizon, and derive a nonlinear integral equation that characterizes the boundaries uniquely. Taken together, these results yield a common decision-theoretic framework and solution theory for a broad class of posterior dynamics that includes the Aldous, Bass, and binary sequential-inference martingales as special cases. Our analysis requires no temporal monotonicity of the running cost and therefore accommodates highly nonmonotone stopping boundaries. In particular, we exhibit an example in which the optimal boundary has no limit as the calendar-time horizon is approached.

_Keywords:_ sequential inference; win-martingales; prediction markets; optimal stopping; free-boundary problems; nonlinear integral equations

_AMS 2020 Subject Classification:_ 60G40, 62L15, 62L10, 60G35, 35R35

## 1\. Introduction

Many high-stakes decisions reduce to a single question: _when should one stop observing and commit to an action?_ Examples range from calling a default, distinguishing noise from informed trading, halting an A/B test, and closing a position, to any setting in which information arrives sequentially and delaying a decision is costly. In each case the decision maker must balance the value of additional information against the cost of waiting.

This paper studies this question in a binary setting. There is an unknown outcome, denoted by θ∈{0,1}\\theta\\in\\{0,1\\}, and the decision maker observes information over time. The natural state variable is the posterior probability that the outcome equals one. Since this posterior is a conditional expectation, it evolves as a bounded martingale taking values in the unit interval. We refer to such processes as posterior win-martingales.

A useful motivation comes from prediction markets. In a prediction market for a binary event, the quoted price changes as information arrives. Abstracting from market frictions, strategic effects, and risk premia, one may, as argued by Wolfers and Zitzewitz \[ [33](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib33 "")\], view this price process as a dynamically updated belief about the terminal outcome. This perspective separates two questions.
First, how should one model the dynamics of an evolving win probability in a way that captures persistent uncertainty? Second, once such a martingale has been chosen, when should a decision maker stop observing it and act?

The first question is closely connected to Aldous’ problem of finding the “ _most exciting game_” \[ [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib1 "")\]. In Aldous’ formulation, one observes a game with a binary terminal outcome, and its excitement is encoded by the evolution of the win probability. A game is exciting when this probability continues to fluctuate and does not quickly settle near zero or one. Recent work on _specific_ distances between continuous-time martingales has given this idea a precise variational formulation. Specific relative entropy, introduced by Gantert in \[ [16](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib16 "")\], and more recent Wasserstein-type variants provide ways of comparing continuous-time martingales even when their path laws are mutually singular; see, for example, \[ [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib3 ""), [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib4 ""), [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib6 ""), [20](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib20 "")\]. Within this framework, variational problems over martingale laws select canonical win-martingales, including the Aldous martingale, Bass-type martingales, and the classical binary sequential-inference posterior.

This paper addresses the second question. We take posterior win-martingales as models for evolving beliefs and study the decision-theoretic problem of when to declare a winner, subject to a running cost and a terminal loss associated with the declaration.
For fixed running cost, terminal loss, and initial belief, the value function is the minimal Bayes risk and gives a decision-theoretic measure of how costly the game is to call optimally.
The difference between the loss from stopping immediately and the optimal value measures the benefit of continued observation. In this sense, it provides a decision-dependent way to compare how difficult different posterior win-martingales are to call, and can be viewed as a complementary decision-theoretic notion of “excitement.”

The classical version of this problem appears in Bayesian sequential testing \[ [32](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib32 "")\]. There, a decision maker observes a signal about an unknown binary state and chooses when to stop and make a terminal decision; see also \[ [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib9 ""), [15](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib15 ""), [17](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib17 ""), [18](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib18 ""), [19](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib19 "")\]. In the Gaussian case, the posterior probability process is a sufficient statistic for the optimal decision rule, and the associated stopping problem has been studied extensively.
Our motivation leads us to take a different approach: we take the win-martingale as given and allow for general running costs and terminal penalties arising from different terminal decision rules. The problem of embedding a given win-martingale as the Bayesian posterior of an explicit sequential experiment is studied in our companion work \[ [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib8 "")\]. A related Bernoulli–Doob representation of bounded martingale diffusions is developed in \[ [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib7 "")\]. The class of win-martingales we study naturally accommodates sequential decision problems in which the posterior reveals the true binary state at a prescribed finite horizon, as in a sports game or election. A specific model with this feature was studied by
Lisovskii \[ [26](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib26 "")\], who considered Bayesian sequential
testing between two possible pinning points of a Brownian bridge. To the best of our knowledge, our analysis provides the first general free-boundary and regularity theory for Bayesian sequential decision problems in which the true state is revealed at a fixed finite time.

We focus on developing the theory for posterior win-martingales whose volatility separates into a product of deterministic time and state-dependent factors. The deterministic time factor describes the rate at which information arrives, and the state-dependent factor describes how the posterior fluctuates at a given belief level. This covers the canonical examples arising from the “most exciting game” and martingale-distance literature, while remaining broad enough to cover more general posterior dynamics.

The decision problem is solved by first reducing it to an associated optimal stopping problem for the win-martingale. A deterministic time change then removes the time-dependent factor from the posterior dynamics and transfers it to the running cost. The original calendar-time cost is thereby converted into an “operational time” cost for observing a time-homogeneous martingale. Consequently, even monotone costs in the original time scale can lead to nonmonotone transformed costs and highly nonmonotone optimal stopping boundaries.

The optimal rule is characterized by a free boundary. While the posterior remains sufficiently uncertain, the decision maker continues observing. Once the posterior becomes sufficiently close to certainty, stopping and making the terminal declaration becomes optimal. We study three settings. In the homogeneous infinite-horizon problem, the transformed running cost is constant and the continuation region is an interval with two constant boundaries. We then analyze the time-inhomogeneous problem on finite and infinite horizons without introducing discounting. Under smoothness, symmetry, and shape assumptions on the problem inputs, the time slices of the continuation region are intervals of the form (1−bT​(t),bT​(t))(1-b\_{T}(t),b\_{T}(t)) for a free boundary bTb\_{T}. We prove that the value function is C1C^{1} globally and C1,2C^{1,2} away from the boundary. We also prove that bTb\_{T} is C1C^{1} before the terminal horizon and derive a nonlinear integral equation that characterizes it uniquely.

The time-inhomogeneous analysis does not assume that the running cost is monotone. This allows for shrinking, expanding, and nonmonotone continuation regions. It also permits the treatment of an oscillatory example in which the calendar-time boundary has no limit as the terminal time is reached. The finite-horizon regularity analysis builds on the probabilistic methods of De Angelis and Stabile \[ [12](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib12 "")\] and De Angelis and Peskir \[ [11](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib11 "")\]. After establishing local Lipschitz regularity and smooth fit in the present two-sided boundary setting, we verify the conditions of De Angelis and Lamberton \[ [10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib10 "")\] to lift the boundary regularity to C1C^{1}. Estimates formed uniformly in the horizon then allow us to pass to the infinite-horizon problem. We also obtain probabilistic representations of the derivatives. For the temporal derivative on finite horizons, we use a technique similar to that of Jaillet, Lamberton, and Lapeyre \[ [22](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib22 "")\].

The contributions of this paper are threefold. First, it places a large class of win-martingales, including those selected by recent martingale-distance problems, into a common sequential decision framework. For fixed running and terminal costs, this provides a decision-theoretic comparison of how difficult the corresponding games are to call. Second, it builds on modern regularity theory to develop a free-boundary theory for two-sided optimal stopping problems with nonmonotone time-inhomogeneous running costs. The analysis covers finite and infinite horizons without discounting and yields both boundary regularity and a unique nonlinear integral-equation characterization. Third, we extend related results in Bayesian sequential testing. Relative to Ekström and Vaicenavicius \[ [14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib14 "")\], we allow a broader class of smooth terminal decision costs, while relative to Campbell and Zhang \[ [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib9 "")\], we allow more general posterior martingales. In both cases, our framework permits time-inhomogeneous running costs without monotonicity assumptions.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2 "2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") introduces the motivating games, reduces the decision-theoretic problem to optimal stopping of the posterior, and performs the deterministic time change. Section [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3 "3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") states the transformed problem and its assumptions, introduces useful transformations, and provides a preliminary analysis. Section [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4 "4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") presents the main structural and regularity results whose proofs are developed in the subsequent sections. Sections [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5 "5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6 "6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") treat the homogeneous and inhomogeneous problems. Section [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S7 "7. Free-Boundary Integral Equation ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") derives the integral-equation characterization. Section [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8 "8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") presents examples and extensions, and Section [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S9 "9. Conclusion ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") concludes.

## 2\. A class of motivating games

We begin with a broad class of sequential prediction problems on a time horizon S∈(0,∞)S\\in(0,\\infty). Consider a filtered probability space (Ω,ℱ,(𝒢s)s∈\[0,S\],ℙ)(\\Omega,\\mathcal{F},(\\mathcal{G}\_{s})\_{s\\in\[0,S\]},\\mathbb{P}) satisfying the usual conditions.
A game produces a 𝒢S\\mathcal{G}\_{S}–measurable binary outcome θ∈{0,1}\\theta\\in\\{0,1\\}, where θ=1\\theta=1 indicates a win and θ=0\\theta=0 a loss.
As the game unfolds, the decision maker observes information over time and updates their belief about the eventual outcome.
The resulting posterior process

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | Πs:=ℙ⁡(θ=1∣𝒢s),s∈\[0,S\],\\Pi\_{s}:=\\mathbb{P}(\\theta=1\\mid\\mathcal{G}\_{s}),\\qquad s\\in\[0,S\], |  |

will be referred to as the _win-martingale_.
Thus Πs\\Pi\_{s} represents the conditional win probability at time ss, given the information available up to that time, as encoded in the filtration (𝒢s)s∈\[0,S\](\\mathcal{G}\_{s})\_{s\\in\[0,S\]}.

Our objective is to decide _when_ to stop observing the game and _what_ prediction to make at that time.
This leads naturally to a sequential decision problem with two components: a stopping rule and a terminal declaration.
Formally, a decision rule is a pair (ν,d)(\\nu,d), where ν≤S\\nu\\leq S is a (𝒢s)s∈\[0,S\](\\mathcal{G}\_{s})\_{s\\in\[0,S\]}–stopping time and dd is a DD-valued 𝒢ν\\mathcal{G}\_{\\nu}–measurable declaration,
where the declaration set is a compact set D⊆\[0,1\]D\\subseteq\[0,1\].
This includes both hard classification, D={0,1}D=\\{0,1\\}, and possibly soft classification, where one reports a probability level, e.g. D=\[0,1\]D=\[0,1\].

To quantify performance, let f:ℝ+→(0,∞)f:\\mathbb{R}\_{+}\\to(0,\\infty) denote the measurable running cost of observation, and let

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | ℓ:{0,1}×D→\[0,∞\]\\ell:\\{0,1\\}\\times D\\to\[0,\\infty\] |  |

be a measurable loss function penalizing the final declaration.
The associated Bayes risk of a decision rule (ν,d)(\\nu,d) is

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| (2.1) |  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0νf⁡(s)​𝑑s+ℓ⁡(θ,d)\].\\mathbb{E}\\!\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\nu}f(s)\\,ds+\\ell(\\theta,d)\\right\]. |  |

The aim is to minimize ( [2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E1 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) over all admissible decision rules for which the expectation is finite to obtain the Bayes rule.

The posterior structure allows the terminal decision to be reduced to a function of the current belief alone.
Indeed, if ν\\nu is fixed and dd is 𝒢ν\\mathcal{G}\_{\\nu}–measurable, then the tower property yields

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝔼⁡\[ℓ⁡(θ,d)\]=𝔼⁡\[h⁡(Πν,d)\],\\mathbb{E}\\!\\left\[\\ell(\\theta,d)\\right\]=\\mathbb{E}\\!\\left\[h(\\Pi\_{\\nu},d)\\right\], |  |

where

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| (2.2) |  | h⁡(π,d):=π​ℓ​(1,d)+(1−π)​ℓ​(0,d),h(\\pi,d):=\\pi\\,\\ell(1,d)+(1-\\pi)\\,\\ell(0,d), |  |

using the convention 0⋅∞=00\\cdot\\infty=0.
For each posterior level π∈\[0,1\]\\pi\\in\[0,1\], the quantity h⁡(π,d)h(\\pi,d) is the conditional expected loss of declaring dd.
If d↦h⁡(π,d)d\\mapsto h(\\pi,d) is lower semicontinuous, then, since π↦h⁡(π,d)\\pi\\mapsto h(\\pi,d) is lower semicontinuous by ( [2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E2 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), and hence, in particular, measurable, one may select a measurable (see e.g. \[ [31](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib31 ""), Theorem 14.37\]) optimal declaration

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | j⁡(π)∈arg​mind⁡(h⁡(π,d)+δD​(d)),j(\\pi)\\in\\argmin\_{d}(h(\\pi,d)+\\delta\_{D}(d)), |  |

and a measurable optimal terminal cost

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | g⁡(π):=infd(h⁡(π,d)+δD​(d))=h⁡(π,j⁡(π)),g(\\pi):=\\inf\_{d}(h(\\pi,d)+\\delta\_{D}(d))=h(\\pi,j(\\pi)), |  |

where δD​(d)=0\\delta\_{D}(d)=0 if d∈Dd\\in D and ∞\\infty otherwise.
Consequently, the original sequential decision problem reduces to the optimal stopping problem

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| (2.3) |  | inf(ν,d)𝔼⁡\[∫0νf⁡(s)​𝑑s+ℓ⁡(θ,d)\]=infν≤S𝔼⁡\[∫0νf⁡(s)​𝑑s+g⁡(Πν)\],\\inf\_{(\\nu,d)}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\nu}f(s)\\,ds+\\ell(\\theta,d)\\right\]=\\inf\_{\\nu\\leq S}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\nu}f(s)\\,ds+g(\\Pi\_{\\nu})\\right\], |  |

where we seek an optimal stopping time ν∗\\nu^{\*} realizing the minimum such that we obtain the Bayes rule (ν∗,j⁡(Πν∗))(\\nu^{\*},j(\\Pi\_{\\nu^{\*}})) minimizing the Bayes risk in ( [2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E1 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).

This formulation covers a range of standard choices. However, our attention will primarily be on the following examples.
Let D=\[0,1\]D=\[0,1\] and consider the squared loss,

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | ℓL2​(θ,d)=(θ−d)2.\\ell\_{L^{2}}(\\theta,d)=(\\theta-d)^{2}. |  |

Then the optimal declaration is j⁡(π)=πj(\\pi)=\\pi and the optimal terminal cost becomes

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | gL2​(π)=π⁡(1−π).g\_{L^{2}}(\\pi)=\\pi(1-\\pi). |  |

Similarly, if D=\[0,1\]D=\[0,1\] with cross-entropy loss,

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | ℓCE​(θ,d)=−θ​log⁡(d)−(1−θ)​log⁡(1−d),\\ell\_{\\text{CE}}(\\theta,d)=-\\theta\\log(d)-(1-\\theta)\\log(1-d), |  |

then the optimal declaration is j⁡(π)=πj(\\pi)=\\pi and
the optimal terminal cost becomes

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | gCE​(π)=−π​log⁡(π)−(1−π)​log⁡(1−π).g\_{\\text{CE}}(\\pi)=-\\pi\\log(\\pi)-(1-\\pi)\\log(1-\\pi). |  |

More generally, different terminal decision rules simply lead to different choices of the terminal cost gg. One may, for example, restrict the declarations to soft classification rules as in \[ [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib9 "")\]. We lay out the assumptions on gg in Section [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.SS2 "3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").

Concrete examples of win-martingales include a family of canonical posterior martingales driven by Brownian noise and pinned to the binary outcome θ\\theta at time SS, stemming from the literature on specific distances between continuous-time martingales. For instance, specific relative entropy selects the _Aldous_ martingale ΠA\\Pi^{A}, whose dynamics are

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | d​ΠsA=sin⁡(π​ΠsA)π​S−s​d​Ws,s∈\[0,S),d\\Pi^{A}\_{s}=\\frac{\\sin(\\pi\\Pi^{A}\_{s})}{\\pi\\sqrt{S-s}}\\,dW\_{s},\\qquad s\\in\[0,S), |  |\
\
while suitable choices of specific pp–Wasserstein distances produce the _Bass_ martingale ΠB\\Pi^{B}\[ [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib2 ""), [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib5 "")\] and the classical _binary sequential-inference_ martingale ΠI\\Pi^{I}:\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | d​ΠsB=φ⁡(Φ−1​(ΠsB))S−s​d​Ws,d​ΠsI=ΠsI​(1−ΠsI)S−s​d​Ws.d\\Pi\_{s}^{B}=\\frac{\\varphi(\\Phi^{-1}(\\Pi\_{s}^{B}))}{\\sqrt{S-s}}\\,dW\_{s},\\qquad d\\Pi\_{s}^{I}=\\frac{\\Pi\_{s}^{I}(1-\\Pi\_{s}^{I})}{\\sqrt{S-s}}\\,dW\_{s}. |  |\
\
Here WW is a Brownian motion and φ,Φ\\varphi,\\Phi are the standard normal pdf/cdf. The Aldous martingale is often presented as the win probability in the “ _most exciting game_,” in the sense that it maximizes “excitement” (equivalently, uncertainty) about the binary outcome under an entropy metric.\
\
To connect this class of games with the analysis in the sequel, we now specialize to win-martingales with separable volatility of the form\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (2.4) |  | d​Πs=ρ⁡(s)​σ​(Πs)​d​Ws,Π0=x∈(0,1),d\\Pi\_{s}=\\rho(s)\\sigma(\\Pi\_{s})\\,dW\_{s},\\qquad\\Pi\_{0}=x\\in(0,1), |  |\
\
for a Brownian motion WW, a continuous deterministic time factor ρ:\[0,S)→(0,∞)\\rho:\[0,S)\\to(0,\\infty), and a state-dependent volatility σ\\sigma such that ( [2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E4 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) admits a strong \[0,1\]\[0,1\]–valued solution.\
At this stage we do not impose detailed assumptions on σ\\sigma; these will be stated in the next section.\
The key point is that ( [2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E4 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) separates the time-inhomogeneous and state-dependent features of the posterior dynamics. The win-martingale Π\\Pi is the only state process and so we will, for simplicity, assume (𝒢s)s∈\[0,S\](\\mathcal{G}\_{s})\_{s\\in\[0,S\]} is the filtration generated by Π\\Pi augmented to satisfy the usual conditions.\
\
This structure makes a time change natural.\
Define\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (2.5) |  | A⁡(s):=∫0sρ2​(v)​𝑑v,A(s):=\\int\_{0}^{s}\\rho^{2}(v)\\,dv, |  |\
\
which we assume to be finite for all s∈\[0,S)s\\in\[0,S), and let\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | T:=lims↗SA⁡(s)∈(0,∞\].T:=\\lim\_{s\\nearrow S}A(s)\\in(0,\\infty\]. |  |\
\
Since AA is strictly increasing, we denote its inverse by Γ:\[0,T)→\[0,S)\\Gamma:\[0,T)\\to\[0,S) and define the time-changed process\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Xt:=ΠΓ⁡(t),t<T.X\_{t}:=\\Pi\_{\\Gamma(t)},\\qquad t<T. |  |\
\
Under the standing assumptions introduced later, the process XX solves the\
time-homogeneous SDE\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (2.6) |  | d​Xt=σ⁡(Xt)​d​BtdX\_{t}=\\sigma(X\_{t})\\,dB\_{t} |  |\
\
for a Brownian motion BB with respect to the operational filtration\
(ℱt)t<T(\\mathcal{F}\_{t})\_{t<T}, where\
ℱt=𝒢Γ⁡(t)\\mathcal{F}\_{t}=\\mathcal{G}\_{\\Gamma(t)} with the usual augmentation. We use\
the convention Γ⁡(T)=S\\Gamma(T)=S. Thus, the deterministic time factor has\
been absorbed into the clock, while the state dependence remains in the\
diffusion coefficient.\
\
If T<∞T<\\infty, the preterminal process XX has the continuous extension\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | XT:=limt↑TXt=lims↑SΠs=ΠS−.X\_{T}:=\\lim\_{t\\uparrow T}X\_{t}=\\lim\_{s\\uparrow S}\\Pi\_{s}=\\Pi\_{S-}. |  |\
\
In general, this left limit need not equal ΠS=θ\\Pi\_{S}=\\theta.\
\
The objective in ( [2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E3 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) transforms accordingly for\
stopping rules that occur before the terminal time. The running cost\
becomes\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (2.7) |  | c⁡(t):=f⁡(Γ⁡(t))​Γ′​(t).c(t):=f(\\Gamma(t))\\Gamma^{\\prime}(t). |  |\
\
If ν<S\\nu<S almost surely and τ=A⁡(ν)\\tau=A(\\nu), then τ<T\\tau<T almost surely.\
Conversely, a stopping time τ<T\\tau<T corresponds to\
ν=Γ⁡(τ)<S\\nu=\\Gamma(\\tau)<S. The time change therefore gives a one-to-one\
correspondence between preterminal stopping rules and preserves their\
costs.\
\
For the canonical examples above, T=∞T=\\infty and\
X∞=θX\_{\\infty}=\\theta almost surely. With the conventions A⁡(S)=∞A(S)=\\infty and\
Γ⁡(∞)=S\\Gamma(\\infty)=S, the correspondence then also includes terminal\
stopping. The time-changed problem is\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (2.8) |  | infτ≥0𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτ)\],\\inf\_{\\tau\\geq 0}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau})\\right\], |  |\
\
where the infimum is taken over extended\
(ℱt)t≥0(\\mathcal{F}\_{t})\_{t\\geq 0}–stopping times for which the expectation is\
finite.\
\
We also study finite-horizon problems of the form ( [2.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E8 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Such a horizon may arise because T<∞T<\\infty or because an exogenous decision deadline is imposed before TT. In either case, we formulate the problem using only the information contained in XX. In particular, when the deadline coincides with the original terminal time SS and T=A⁡(S)<∞T=A(S)<\\infty, we tacitly assume that no additional information is revealed at SS, so that ΠS−=ΠS\\Pi\_{S-}=\\Pi\_{S}. We set aside the exceptional case in which additional information is revealed discontinuously at the terminal time, so that ΠS−≠ΠS\\Pi\_{S-}\\neq\\Pi\_{S}. Such a terminal information jump leads to a different decision problem that is not considered here.\
\
More generally, by restarting the problem from time tt and utilizing the strong Markov property, one is led to the family\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (2.9) |  | VT​(t,x):=infτ≤T−t𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\],V\_{T}(t,x):=\\inf\_{\\tau\\leq T-t}\\mathbb{E}\\!\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau}^{x})\\right\], |  |\
\
where XxX^{x} solves ( [2.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E6 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) with X0x=xX^{x}\_{0}=x, and the infimum is taken over stopping times with respect to the usual augmentation of the filtration generated by XxX^{x}. The constraint τ≤T−t\\tau\\leq T-t is interpreted as τ≤∞\\tau\\leq\\infty when T=∞T=\\infty, and we minimize over stopping times for which the expectation is finite.\
\
Thus, sequential prediction in a game with binary outcome naturally leads to an optimal stopping problem for a posterior martingale, and for the class ( [2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E4 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) this problem can be rewritten, after a deterministic time change, as an optimal stopping problem for a time-homogeneous diffusion.\
The next section takes the transformed problem variables (σ,c,g)(\\sigma,c,g) as primitives and formalizes the assumptions under which the transformed problem is well posed.\
\
## 3\. Setup and Preliminary Analysis\
\
In this section we formalize the class of optimal stopping problems studied throughout the paper, either stemming from motivating problems such as those in Section [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2 "2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") or in their own right. We first introduce the underlying state process and the associated infinite-horizon stopping problem. We then collect the assumptions and present different formulations of associated problems used in the subsequent analysis. Finally, we derive a number of preliminary analytical results.\
\
### 3.1. Problem formulation\
\
Let (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\\Omega,\\mathcal{F},\\mathbb{P}) be a probability space supporting a standard Brownian motion B=(Bt)t≥0B=(B\_{t})\_{t\\geq 0}, and let 𝔽=(ℱt)t≥0\\mathbb{F}=(\\mathcal{F}\_{t})\_{t\\geq 0} denote the usual augmentation of the natural filtration of BB.\
\
For each x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1), we consider a process Xx=(Xtx)t≥0X^{x}=(X\_{t}^{x})\_{t\\geq 0} solving the stochastic differential equation\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.1) |  | d​Xtx=σ⁡(Xtx)​d​Bt,X0x=x.dX\_{t}^{x}=\\sigma(X\_{t}^{x})\\,dB\_{t},\\qquad X\_{0}^{x}=x. |  |\
\
We will impose assumptions below ensuring that ( [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E1 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) admits a unique strong solution and that the boundary points {0,1}\\{0,1\\} are inaccessible in finite time. For x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\} we extend the definition by letting the process remain constant, that is, Xtx=xX\_{t}^{x}=x for all t≥0t\\geq 0.\
\
For x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1), under these assumptions XxX^{x} is a time-homogeneous martingale diffusion taking values in \[0,1\]\[0,1\] and absorbed at the endpoints. Since σ\\sigma depends on the state alone, in line with the terminology of \[ [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib8 "")\], we call XxX^{x} an _autonomous win-martingale_. However, since autonomy is assumed throughout the remainder of the paper, we suppress the qualifier “autonomous” in what follows.\
\
We let ℒ\\mathcal{L} denote the infinitesimal generator associated with XxX^{x}, given by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ℒ=12σ2(x)∂x​x.\\mathcal{L}=\\frac{1}{2}\\sigma^{2}(x)\\partial\_{xx}. |  |\
\
Let c:ℝ+→(0,∞)c:\\mathbb{R}\_{+}\\to(0,\\infty) be a measurable running cost and let g:\[0,1\]→ℝ+g:\[0,1\]\\to\\mathbb{R}\_{+} be a bounded measurable terminal cost. The infinite-horizon optimal stopping problem is\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.2) |  | V⁡(t,x):=infτ𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\],V(t,x):=\\inf\_{\\tau}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau}^{x})\\right\], |  |\
\
where the infimum is taken over all 𝔽\\mathbb{F}–stopping times τ\\tau such that the expectation is finite. We denote this set by 𝒯(t)\\mathcal{T}^{(t)}. In particular, since gg is bounded, every admissible stopping time τ∈𝒯(t)\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}^{(t)} satisfies\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u\]<∞.\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du\\right\]<\\infty. |  |\
\
Similarly, we also define 𝒯\\mathcal{T} as the set of stopping times such that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(u)​𝑑u\]<∞.\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(u)\\,du\\right\]<\\infty. |  |\
\
It clearly holds that 𝒯(t)⊆𝒯\\mathcal{T}^{(t)}\\subseteq\\mathcal{T} when cc is integrable on \[0,T1\]\[0,T\_{1}\] for any T1≥0T\_{1}\\geq 0.\
\
We denote the state space by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒮:=ℝ+×(0,1).\\mathcal{S}:=\\mathbb{R}\_{+}\\times(0,1). |  |\
\
Furthermore, we define the continuation and stopping regions by\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.3) |  | 𝒞:={(t,x)∈𝒮:V⁡(t,x)<g⁡(x)},𝒟:={(t,x)∈𝒮:V⁡(t,x)=g⁡(x)}.\\mathcal{C}:=\\{(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S}:V(t,x)<g(x)\\},\\qquad\\mathcal{D}:=\\{(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S}:V(t,x)=g(x)\\}. |  |\
\
For convenience we also define the extended state space by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒮¯=ℝ+×\[0,1\].\\overline{\\mathcal{S}}=\\mathbb{R}\_{+}\\times\[0,1\]. |  |\
\
However, we consider points (t,x)(t,x) to be in 𝒮\\mathcal{S} unless explicitly stated.\
\
### 3.2. Assumptions\
\
We now collect the assumptions used throughout the paper.\
We separate the baseline assumptions needed for the basic formulation and qualitative structure from the extended regularity assumptions needed for the finer analysis of the time-inhomogeneous problem.\
\
###### Assumption A(Baseline assumptions).\
\
Assume the following.\
\
1. (i)\
\
\
The function σ:\[0,1\]→ℝ+\\sigma:\[0,1\]\\to\\mathbb{R}\_{+} is C⁡(\[0,1\])C(\[0,1\]), Liploc​((,,,))\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}((0,1)), and satisfies σ⁡(x)=0\\sigma(x)=0 if and only if x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\}.\
Moreover, σ−2∈Lloc1​((0,1))\\sigma^{-2}\\in L^{1}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}((0,1)) and, for any a∈(0,1)a\\in(0,1),\
\
\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.4) |  | ∫ax∫ay1σ2​(z)​𝑑z​𝑑y→∞as ​x→0​ and as ​x→1.\\int\_{a}^{x}\\int\_{a}^{y}\\frac{1}{\\sigma^{2}(z)}\\,dz\\,dy\\to\\infty\\qquad\\text{as }x\\to 0\\text{ and as }x\\to 1. |  |\
\
2. (ii)\
\
\
The running cost c:ℝ+→(0,∞)c:\\mathbb{R}\_{+}\\to(0,\\infty) is C1​(\[0,∞))C^{1}(\[0,\\infty)) and there exists a constant K>0K>0 such that\
\
\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.5) |  | \|c′​(t)c⁡(t)\|≤K,t≥0.\\left\|\\frac{c^{\\prime}(t)}{c(t)}\\right\|\\leq K,\\qquad t\\geq 0. |  |\
\
3. (iii)\
\
\
The terminal cost g:\[0,1\]→ℝ+g:\[0,1\]\\to\\mathbb{R}\_{+} is C⁡(\[0,1\])C(\[0,1\]), C2​((,,,))C^{2}((0,1)), concave, and g⁡(x)=0g(x)=0 if and only if x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\}.\
\
4. (iv)\
\
\
There exists x0∈(0,1)x\_{0}\\in(0,1) such that ℒ​g\\mathcal{L}g is strictly decreasing on (0,x0)(0,x\_{0}) and strictly increasing on (x0,1)(x\_{0},1).\
\
5. (v)\
\
\
For all t≥0t\\geq 0, it holds that\
\
\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ℒ​g​(x0)+c⁡(t)<0.\\mathcal{L}g(x\_{0})+c(t)<0. |  |\
\
\
Assumption [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") fixes the class of posterior diffusions considered in the paper, namely, posterior diffusions with unique strong solutions such that the process stays in (0,1)(0,1) in finite time, see Proposition [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1. ‣ 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). Assumptions [A.(ii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i2 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and [A.(iii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i3 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") state the basic assumptions on the running cost and the terminal cost. The concavity of gg and its vanishing on the boundary in Assumption [A.(iii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i3 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") are natural in the processes arising from Section [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2 "2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). Indeed, since π↦h⁡(π,d)\\pi\\mapsto h(\\pi,d) is affine for every fixed dd, the induced terminal cost gg is the pointwise minimum of affine functions, and hence concave.\
\
The second part of Assumption [A.(ii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i2 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")\
gives an exponential bound for the growth of the cost. Namely, we have \|∂tlog⁡(c⁡(v))\|≤K\\lvert\\partial\_{t}\\log(c(v))\\rvert\\leq K for v≥0v\\geq 0 and integration gives\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|log⁡c⁡(v+u)c⁡(v)\|≤\|u\|​K\\left\\lvert\\log\\frac{c(v+u)}{c(v)}\\right\\rvert\\leq\\lvert u\\rvert K |  |\
\
where v+u≥0v+u\\geq 0. By exponentiation, we obtain the exponential growth condition\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.6) |  | c⁡(v)​e−K​\|u\|≤c⁡(v+u)≤c⁡(v)​eK​\|u\|.c(v)e^{-K\\lvert u\\rvert}\\leq c(v+u)\\leq c(v)e^{K\\lvert u\\rvert}. |  |\
\
Thus, we get the local (in uu) estimate\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.7) |  | \|c′​(v+u)\|≤K​c​(v+u)≤K′​c​(v),\|c^{\\prime}(v+u)\|\\leq Kc(v+u)\\leq K^{\\prime}c(v), |  |\
\
where for, say, \|u\|≤1\|u\|\\leq 1, K′:=K​eKK^{\\prime}:=Ke^{K}.\
Equation ( [3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E5 "In A.(ii) ‣ Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) enables control of the running cost useful for basic continuity results such as Proposition [3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition 3.5. ‣ 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
\
Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") ensures a unimodal structure of the stopping problem. Namely, the closer the process is to the boundary {0,1}\\{0,1\\}, the more likely it should be that we stop. This can be realized through the Lagrange formulation of the problem given in Proposition [3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6. ‣ 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). Finally, Assumption [A.(v)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i5 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") ensures a nonempty continuation set, while also ensuring a bounded running cost.\
\
For the time-inhomogeneous problem we impose the following additional assumptions.\
\
###### Assumption B(Extended assumptions).\
\
In addition to Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), assume the following:\
\
1. (i)\
\
\
The coefficients satisfy σ∈C2​((0,1))\\sigma\\in C^{2}((0,1)) and g∈C3​((,,,))g\\in C^{3}((0,1)).\
\
2. (ii)\
\
\
For the point x0x\_{0} appearing in Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"),\
\
\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ∂xℒ​g​(x)<0on ​(0,x0),∂xℒ​g​(x)>0on ​(x0,1).\\partial\_{x}\\mathcal{L}g(x)<0\\quad\\text{on }(0,x\_{0}),\\qquad\\partial\_{x}\\mathcal{L}g(x)>0\\quad\\text{on }(x\_{0},1). |  |\
\
3. (iii)\
\
\
Either\
\
\
\
1. (a)\
\
\
      ‖g′‖∞<∞\\\|g^{\\prime}\\\|\_{\\infty}<\\infty, or\
\
2. (b)\
\
\
      there exists m>0m>0 such that c⁡(t)≥mc(t)\\geq m for all t≥0t\\geq 0.\
\
\
4. (iv)\
\
\
The functions σ\\sigma, gg, and ℒ​g\\mathcal{L}g are symmetric around 1/21/2.\
\
5. (v)\
\
\
The stochastic flow derivative (∂xXtx)t∈\[0,T1\](\\partial\_{x}X\_{t}^{x})\_{t\\in\[0,T\_{1}\]}, defined below in ( [3.14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E14 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), is a true martingale for every x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1) and T1<∞T\_{1}<\\infty.\
\
\
Assumption [B.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i1 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") ensures enough differentiability to apply modern regularity techniques in optimal stopping. Assumption [B.(ii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i2 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") strengthens Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), removing stationary points.\
The assumptions in Assumption [B.(iii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") are used to make derivative estimates of the value function finite.\
\
Our main interest lies in symmetric games, and Assumption [B.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i4 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") will simplify several later arguments. Most of the analysis can be directly extended to asymmetric problems, but doing so requires additional bookkeeping.\
Finally, Assumption [B.(v)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i5 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") can be verified in several standard ways. For many examples, such as the classical sequential testing problem with σ⁡(x)=x⁡(1−x)\\sigma(x)=x(1-x), the condition ‖σ′‖∞<∞\\\|\\sigma^{\\prime}\\\|\_{\\infty}<\\infty is enough to verify Novikov’s condition. For other examples, such as the Bass win-martingale, Novikov’s condition may be less tractable. A useful alternative sufficient condition is linear growth of the Lamperti drift given in ( [3.11](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E11 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")),\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|μ⁡(l)\|≤k⁡(1+\|l\|),\|\\mu(l)\|\\leq k(1+\|l\|), |  |\
\
for some constant k>0k>0, which implies the martingale property by a Beneš-type argument; see e.g. \[ [25](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib25 ""), Theorem 4.1\]. This indeed holds for the Bass win-martingale.\
\
### 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup\
\
We first record the basic consequences of the setup and basic assumptions.\
\
###### Proposition 3.1.\
\
Under Assumption [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), for every x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1) the SDE ( [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E1 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) admits a unique strong solution. Moreover, the boundary points {0,1}\\{0,1\\} are inaccessible in finite time and\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | X∞x:=limt→∞Xtx∈{0,1}X\_{\\infty}^{x}:=\\lim\_{t\\to\\infty}X\_{t}^{x}\\in\\{0,1\\} |  |\
\
almost surely.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Since σ∈Liploc​((0,1))\\sigma\\in\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}((0,1)), a standard localization argument yields existence and pathwise uniqueness of XxX^{x} up to the first exit time from (0,1)(0,1). The Feller test ( [3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E4 "In A.(i) ‣ Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) implies that the boundary points are inaccessible in finite time. Since XxX^{x} is a bounded martingale in \[0,1\]\[0,1\], it converges almost surely to a random variable in \[0,1\]\[0,1\]. Since the process is in natural scale, the scale function is finite at 00 and 11, so the limit must lie in {0,1}\\{0,1\\}. See e.g. \[ [23](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib23 ""), Chapter 5\].\
∎\
\
As with the extension of XxX^{x} to x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\}, we also extend the definition of the value function to the extended state space 𝒮¯\\overline{\\mathcal{S}} by setting\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V⁡(t,x)≡0V(t,x)\\equiv 0 |  |\
\
on (t,x)∈𝒮¯∖𝒮(t,x)\\in\\overline{\\mathcal{S}}\\setminus\\mathcal{S} under Assumption [A.(iii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i3 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") since g⁡(x)=0g(x)=0 if x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\}.\
Let 𝔽x:=(ℱtx)t≥0\\mathbb{F}^{x}:=(\\mathcal{F}\_{t}^{x})\_{t\\geq 0} denote the usual augmentation of the natural filtration of XxX^{x}.\
\
###### Proposition 3.2.\
\
Under Assumption [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), for every x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1), the filtrations 𝔽\\mathbb{F} and 𝔽x\\mathbb{F}^{x} coincide.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Fix a t<∞t<\\infty and x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1). Since Xsx∉{0,1}X\_{s}^{x}\\notin\\{0,1\\} for all s≤ts\\leq t almost surely, it follows that σ⁡(Xsx)≠0\\sigma(X\_{s}^{x})\\neq 0, and thus\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Bt=∫0t1σ⁡(Xsx)​d​XsxB\_{t}=\\int\_{0}^{t}\\frac{1}{\\sigma(X\_{s}^{x})}\\,dX\_{s}^{x} |  |\
\
by ( [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E1 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Hence BtB\_{t} is ℱtx\\mathcal{F}\_{t}^{x}–measurable. Conversely, XxX^{x} is adapted to 𝔽\\mathbb{F} by ( [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E1 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Hence, the raw filtration generated by BB is contained in the raw filtration generated by XxX^{x},\
and therefore their usual augmentations coincide.\
∎\
\
###### Proposition 3.3.\
\
Under Assumption [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), there exists a jointly measurable version of (t,x,ω)↦Xtx​(ω)(t,x,\\omega)\\mapsto X^{x}\_{t}(\\omega) such that on compacts of 𝒮\\mathcal{S},\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | (t,x)↦Xtx(t,x)\\mapsto X^{x}\_{t} |  |\
\
is continuous almost surely.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Since σ∈Liploc​((0,1))\\sigma\\in\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}((0,1)), the SDE ( [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E1 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) admits a unique strong solution up to the first exit time from (0,1)(0,1). Furthermore, the solution has continuous paths. By Proposition [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1. ‣ 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), the exit time is infinite due to the Feller condition. Using \[ [30](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib30 ""), Theorem V.37\], the flow x↦Xtxx\\mapsto X^{x}\_{t} is continuous in the topology of uniform convergence on compacts, almost surely. Thus, joint continuity holds almost surely. Since (t,x,ω)↦Xtx​(ω)(t,x,\\omega)\\mapsto X^{x}\_{t}(\\omega) is jointly almost surely continuous in the first two arguments and measurable in the last argument, a standard modification argument yields a Carathéodory function. Thus, it is jointly measurable in (t,x,ω)(t,x,\\omega).\
∎\
\
###### Proposition 3.4.\
\
Under Assumption [A.(ii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i2 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), the set of admissible strategies obeys 𝒯(t)=𝒯\\mathcal{T}^{(t)}=\\mathcal{T}.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
We already have 𝒯(t)⊆𝒯\\mathcal{T}^{(t)}\\subseteq\\mathcal{T}. For the converse, take τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T} and any t≥0t\\geq 0. Then, by ( [3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E6 "In 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we have c⁡(t+u)≤eK​t​c​(u)c(t+u)\\leq e^{Kt}c(u) for any u≥0u\\geq 0. Hence,\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u\]≤eK​t​𝔼​\[∫0τc⁡(u)​𝑑u\]<∞\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du\\right\]\\leq e^{Kt}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(u)\\,du\\right\]<\\infty |  |\
\
by assumption.\
∎\
\
###### Proposition 3.5.\
\
Under Assumptions [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), [A.(ii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i2 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), and [A.(iii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i3 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), for every fixed admissible stopping time τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}, the map\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | (t,x)↦𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\](t,x)\\mapsto\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau}^{x})\\right\] |  |\
\
is continuous on 𝒮¯\\overline{\\mathcal{S}}.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Since the expression is additively separable, it suffices to show continuity of\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | t↦𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u\],andx↦𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτx)\].t\\mapsto\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du\\right\],\\qquad\\text{and}\\qquad x\\mapsto\\mathbb{E}\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\]. |  |\
\
For temporal continuity, if t∈\[0,T1\]t\\in\[0,T\_{1}\], then c⁡(t+u)≤eK​T1​c​(u)c(t+u)\\leq e^{KT\_{1}}c(u) by ( [3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E6 "In 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Thus, since τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}, we can apply dominated convergence to conclude temporal continuity.\
\
For spatial continuity we first take (xn)⊂(0,1)(x\_{n})\\subset(0,1) such that xn→x∈(0,1)x\_{n}\\to x\\in(0,1). Then, pathwise on {τ<∞}\\{\\tau<\\infty\\}, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Xτ⁡(ω)xn​(ω)→Xτ⁡(ω)x​(ω)X^{x\_{n}}\_{\\tau(\\omega)}(\\omega)\\to X^{x}\_{\\tau(\\omega)}(\\omega) |  |\
\
for almost all ω∈Ω\\omega\\in\\Omega by Proposition [3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 3.3. ‣ 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). On {τ=∞}\\{\\tau=\\infty\\} we have Xτy=X∞y∈{0,1}X^{y}\_{\\tau}=X^{y}\_{\\infty}\\in\\{0,1\\} for all y∈(0,1)y\\in(0,1), hence\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | g⁡(Xτxn)=0=g⁡(Xτx)g(X^{x\_{n}}\_{\\tau})=0=g(X^{x}\_{\\tau}) |  |\
\
for all nn. Combining, we get for any stopping time that g⁡(Xτxn)→g⁡(Xτx)g(X^{x\_{n}}\_{\\tau})\\to g(X^{x}\_{\\tau}) almost surely. Since gg is bounded, dominated convergence yields the claim for x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1).\
\
To extend to the endpoints we note that by continuity of gg we can for every ε>0\\varepsilon>0 find a δ>0\\delta>0 such that g⁡(x)<εg(x)<\\varepsilon for all x∈\[0,δ\]x\\in\[0,\\delta\]. Since XxX^{x} is a bounded martingale, optional sampling yields 𝔼⁡\[Xτx\]=x\\mathbb{E}\[X^{x}\_{\\tau}\]=x for possibly infinite stopping times τ\\tau. Markov’s inequality yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ℙ⁡(Xτx>δ)≤xδ,\\mathbb{P}(X^{x}\_{\\tau}>\\delta)\\leq\\frac{x}{\\delta}, |  |\
\
and hence\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτx)\]≤ε​ℙ​(Xτx≤δ)+‖g‖∞​ℙ​(Xτx>δ)≤ε+‖g‖∞​xδ.\\mathbb{E}\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\]\\leq\\varepsilon\\mathbb{P}(X^{x}\_{\\tau}\\leq\\delta)+\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty}\\mathbb{P}(X^{x}\_{\\tau}>\\delta)\\leq\\varepsilon+\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty}\\frac{x}{\\delta}. |  |\
\
Taking the limit we get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 0≤lim supx↘0𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτx)\]≤ε.0\\leq\\limsup\_{x\\searrow 0}\\mathbb{E}\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\]\\leq\\varepsilon. |  |\
\
Since ε\\varepsilon was arbitrary, we get spatial continuity at 00. A symmetric argument at 11 yields the claim.\
∎\
\
### 3.4. Transformations and subproblems\
\
Different transformations of the problem yield insights into the structure of the solutions. A standard transformation is the Lagrange formulation of the problem defined by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W⁡(t,x):=V⁡(t,x)−g⁡(x).W(t,x):=V(t,x)-g(x). |  |\
\
Then Dynkin’s formula yields\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.8) |  | W⁡(t,x)=infτ𝔼⁡\[∫0τ(c⁡(t+u)+ℒ​g​(Xux))​𝑑u\],W(t,x)=\\inf\_{\\tau}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}\\big(c(t+u)+\\mathcal{L}g(X\_{u}^{x})\\big)\\,du\\right\], |  |\
\
by the following proposition.\
\
###### Proposition 3.6.\
\
Under Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), it holds that\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.9) |  | 𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτx)\]−g⁡(x)=𝔼⁡\[∫0τℒ​g​(Xux)​𝑑u\],\\mathbb{E}\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\]-g(x)=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}\\mathcal{L}g(X^{x}\_{u})\\,du\\right\], |  |\
\
for any stopping time τ\\tau adapted to 𝔽\\mathbb{F}.\
Specifically, the representation ( [3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E8 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) holds.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Fix x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1) and an 𝔽\\mathbb{F}–stopping time τ\\tau. Since g′g^{\\prime} may be unbounded near\
the endpoints and τ\\tau may be unbounded, we need to localize in space and time, respectively. For mm large enough that\
x∈(1/m,1−1/m)x\\in(1/m,1-1/m), set\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ηm:=inf{u≥0:Xux∉(1/m,1−1/m)}.\\eta\_{m}:=\\inf\\{u\\geq 0:X\_{u}^{x}\\notin(1/m,1-1/m)\\}. |  |\
\
By Proposition [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1. ‣ 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), ηm↗∞\\eta\_{m}\\nearrow\\infty almost surely. Since\
g∈C2​(1/m,1−1/m)g\\in C^{2}(1/m,1-1/m) with bounded derivatives on compact subintervals,\
Dynkin’s formula applied to τ∧n∧ηm\\tau\\wedge n\\wedge\\eta\_{m} gives\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτ∧n∧ηmx)−g⁡(x)\]=𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∧n∧ηmℒ​g​(Xux)​𝑑u\].\\mathbb{E}\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau\\wedge n\\wedge\\eta\_{m}})-g(x)\]=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau\\wedge n\\wedge\\eta\_{m}}\\mathcal{L}g(X\_{u}^{x})\\,du\\right\]. |  |\
\
Letting m→∞m\\to\\infty, the left-hand side converges by dominated convergence,\
because gg is bounded and continuous. On the right-hand side, concavity of\
gg implies ℒ​g≤0\\mathcal{L}g\\leq 0, so monotone convergence yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτ∧nx)−g⁡(x)\]=𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∧nℒ​g​(Xux)​𝑑u\].\\mathbb{E}\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau\\wedge n})-g(x)\]=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau\\wedge n}\\mathcal{L}g(X\_{u}^{x})\\,du\\right\]. |  |\
\
Finally let n→∞n\\to\\infty. Again the left-hand side converges by dominated\
convergence, using the convention Xτx=X∞xX^{x}\_{\\tau}=X^{x}\_{\\infty} on {τ=∞}\\{\\tau=\\infty\\},\
while the right-hand side converges by monotone convergence since\
ℒ​g≤0\\mathcal{L}g\\leq 0. Hence, we arrive at ( [3.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E9 "In Proposition 3.6. ‣ 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
Adding the time cost and taking the infimum over stopping times τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}, we arrive at ( [3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E8 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
∎\
\
Although the formulations in (t,x)(t,x)–coordinates are natural, the state dependence of the diffusion coefficient complicates the analysis. To work with a unit diffusion coefficient, we introduce the Lamperti transformation. Fix a reference point z∈(0,1)z\\in(0,1), say z=1/2z=1/2 under the symmetry assumption [B.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i4 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), and define\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Ψ⁡(x):=∫zx1σ⁡(y)​𝑑y,\\Psi(x):=\\int\_{z}^{x}\\frac{1}{\\sigma(y)}\\,dy, |  |\
\
for x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1).\
Then Ψ\\Psi is strictly increasing, and hence invertible on its image. Writing l=Ψ⁡(x)l=\\Psi(x), define the transformed process\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Ltl:=Ψ⁡(Xtx).L\_{t}^{l}:=\\Psi(X\_{t}^{x}). |  |\
\
As in the original coordinates we formally extend the process and the mapping at the endpoints by Ltl≡lL^{l}\_{t}\\equiv l for all tt when l=Ψ⁡(x):=limy→xΨ⁡(y)l=\\Psi(x):=\\lim\_{y\\to x}\\Psi(y) for x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\}, where the limit may be infinite.\
Under Assumption [B.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i1 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") on σ\\sigma, Itô’s formula shows that LlL^{l} solves\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.10) |  | d​Ltl=μ⁡(Ltl)​d​t+d​Bt,L0l=l,dL\_{t}^{l}=\\mu(L\_{t}^{l})\\,dt+dB\_{t},\\qquad L\_{0}^{l}=l, |  |\
\
where\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.11) |  | μ⁡(l)=−σ′​(Ψ−1​(l))2.\\mu(l)=-\\frac{\\sigma^{\\prime}(\\Psi^{-1}(l))}{2}. |  |\
\
The transformed state space is\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒮~:=ℝ+×Ψ⁡((0,1)),\\tilde{\\mathcal{S}}:=\\mathbb{R}\_{+}\\times\\Psi((0,1)), |  |\
\
and we note that the assumptions in Section [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.SS2 "3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") do not impose any assumptions on the size of Ψ⁡((,,,))\\Psi((0,1)), other than it being a nonempty interval.\
\
Define the transformed value function\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V~​(t,l):=infτ𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g~​(Lτl)\]=V⁡(t,Ψ−1​(l)),\\tilde{V}(t,l):=\\inf\_{\\tau}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+\\tilde{g}(L\_{\\tau}^{l})\\right\]=V(t,\\Psi^{-1}(l)), |  |\
\
where the infimum is taken over admissible 𝔽\\mathbb{F} stopping times and g~​(l):=g⁡(Ψ−1​(l))\\tilde{g}(l):=g(\\Psi^{-1}(l)). Similarly, define\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W~​(t,l):=infτ𝔼⁡\[∫0τ(c⁡(t+u)+ℒ~​g~​(Lul))​𝑑u\]=W⁡(t,Ψ−1​(l)),\\tilde{W}(t,l):=\\inf\_{\\tau}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}\\big(c(t+u)+\\tilde{\\mathcal{L}}\\tilde{g}(L\_{u}^{l})\\big)\\,du\\right\]=W(t,\\Psi^{-1}(l)), |  |\
\
where the infinitesimal generator of LlL^{l} is\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ℒ~=μ(l)∂l+12∂l​l.\\tilde{\\mathcal{L}}=\\mu(l)\\partial\_{l}+\\frac{1}{2}\\partial\_{ll}. |  |\
\
Similarly, we also denote the Lamperti-transformed sets by 𝒞~\\tilde{\\mathcal{C}}, 𝒟~\\tilde{\\mathcal{D}} and so on, meaning, e.g., (t,l)∈𝒞~(t,l)\\in\\tilde{\\mathcal{C}} if and only if (t,Ψ−1​(l))∈𝒞(t,\\Psi^{-1}(l))\\in\\mathcal{C}.\
\
Through the Lamperti transformation, the derivative of the stochastic flow can easily be derived under Assumptions [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and [B.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i1 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") on σ\\sigma. Differentiating the integrated version of ( [3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E10 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) gives\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ∂lLtl=1+∫0tμ′​(Lul)​∂lLul​𝑑u,\\partial\_{l}L\_{t}^{l}=1+\\int\_{0}^{t}\\mu^{\\prime}(L\_{u}^{l})\\partial\_{l}L\_{u}^{l}\\,du, |  |\
\
for l∈Ψ⁡((,,,))l\\in\\Psi((0,1)), and hence\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.12) |  | ∂lLtl=exp⁡(∫0tμ′​(Lul)​𝑑u),\\partial\_{l}L\_{t}^{l}=\\exp\\left(\\int\_{0}^{t}\\mu^{\\prime}(L\_{u}^{l})\\,du\\right), |  |\
\
up to the first time Ltl∈Ψ⁡({0,1})L^{l}\_{t}\\in\\Psi(\\{0,1\\}), which does not happen in finite time.\
\
By the chain rule this yields\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.13) |  | ∂xXtx=σ⁡(Xtx)σ⁡(x)exp(−∫0tσ′′​(Xux)​σ​(Xux)2du),\\partial\_{x}X\_{t}^{x}=\\frac{\\sigma(X\_{t}^{x})}{\\sigma(x)}\\exp\\left(-\\int\_{0}^{t}\\frac{\\sigma^{\\prime\\prime}(X\_{u}^{x})\\sigma(X\_{u}^{x})}{2}\\,du\\right), |  |\
\
and equivalently\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.14) |  | d⁡(∂xXtx)=σ′​(Xtx)​∂xXtx​d​Bt,∂xX0x=1.d(\\partial\_{x}X\_{t}^{x})=\\sigma^{\\prime}(X\_{t}^{x})\\partial\_{x}X\_{t}^{x}\\,dB\_{t},\\qquad\\partial\_{x}X\_{0}^{x}=1. |  |\
\
It should be clear from the explicit expression ( [3.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E13 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) that joint continuity and measurability statements like those in Proposition [3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 3.3. ‣ 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") can also be made for (t,x,ω)↦∂xXtx​(ω)(t,x,\\omega)\\mapsto\\partial\_{x}X\_{t}^{x}(\\omega).\
\
Although the main motivation comes from exciting games that naturally lead to infinite-horizon problems, the regularity analysis is more conveniently carried out for finite-horizon problems. We therefore consider, for each 0<T<∞0<T<\\infty, the family\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.15) |  | VT​(t,x):=infτ∈𝒯T−t𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\],V\_{T}(t,x):=\\inf\_{\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t}}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau}^{x})\\right\], |  |\
\
where\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒯T−t:={τ∈𝒯:τ≤T−t}.\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t}:=\\{\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}:\\tau\\leq T-t\\}. |  |\
\
Likewise, we denote the finite-horizon versions of the infinite-horizon quantities by the TT subscript, e.g., 𝒞T\\mathcal{C}\_{T} and W~T\\tilde{W}\_{T} for the finite-horizon continuation region and the finite-horizon Lamperti-transformed Lagrange formulation, respectively. Formally, we also write V∞=VV\_{\\infty}=V, etc. for the infinite-horizon problem. Specifically, we denote the state space by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒮T:=\[0,T)×(0,1),\\mathcal{S}\_{T}:=\[0,T)\\times(0,1), |  |\
\
and the extended state space by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒮¯T:=\[0,T\]×\[0,1\].\\overline{\\mathcal{S}}\_{T}:=\[0,T\]\\times\[0,1\]. |  |\
\
As for the infinite-horizon state space, we will consider points (t,x)(t,x) to be in the state space 𝒮T\\mathcal{S}\_{T} unless explicitly stated. We formally set\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | VT​(t,x):=g​(x)V\_{T}(t,x):=g(x) |  |\
\
for (t,x)∈𝒮¯T∖𝒮T(t,x)\\in\\overline{\\mathcal{S}}\_{T}\\setminus\\mathcal{S}\_{T}.\
\
When no ambiguity arises, we suppress the dependence of the admissible class of stopping times on the horizon. We emphasize that the running cost cc remains defined on all of ℝ+\\mathbb{R}\_{+}.\
\
### 3.5. Preliminary Analysis\
\
We next address initial properties of the value function and the optimal stopping rule. The following results hold for T≤∞T\\leq\\infty unless otherwise stated.\
\
###### Proposition 3.7.\
\
If Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and T≤∞T\\leq\\infty hold, then VTV\_{T} is upper semicontinuous, 𝒞T\\mathcal{C}\_{T} is open, and 𝒟T\\mathcal{D}\_{T} is closed. Furthermore, the stopping time\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (3.16) |  | τT∗​(t,x):=inf{0≤s<T−t:(t+s,Xsx)∈𝒟T}∧(T−t)\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x):=\\inf\\{0\\leq s<T-t:(t+s,X\_{s}^{x})\\in\\mathcal{D}\_{T}\\}\\wedge(T-t) |  |\
\
is the smallest optimal stopping time for T<∞T<\\infty.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
If T=∞T=\\infty, taking the infimum over continuous functions indexed by a family τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T} induces an upper semicontinuous function VTV\_{T}. For T<∞T<\\infty, fix (t,x)∈𝒮T(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S}\_{T} and ε>0\\varepsilon>0. Choose an ε\\varepsilon–optimal time τ∈𝒯T−t\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t} such that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | VT​(t,x)+ε>𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\].V\_{T}(t,x)+\\varepsilon>\\mathbb{E}\\!\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau}^{x})\\right\]. |  |\
\
Let (tn,xn)→(t,x)(t\_{n},x\_{n})\\to(t,x) and define τn:=τ∧(T−tn)\\tau\_{n}:=\\tau\\wedge(T-t\_{n}), for which τn→τ\\tau\_{n}\\to\\tau almost surely.\
Reverse Fatou’s lemma and continuity yield\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | lim supn→∞𝔼⁡\[∫0τnc⁡(tn+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτnxn)\]≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\],\\limsup\_{n\\to\\infty}\\mathbb{E}\\!\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau\_{n}}c(t\_{n}+u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau\_{n}}^{x\_{n}})\\right\]\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\!\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X\_{\\tau}^{x})\\right\], |  |\
\
where we use that on a finite horizon the integrand can easily be dominated.\
Since VT​(tn,xn)V\_{T}(t\_{n},x\_{n}) is the optimal value, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | lim supn→∞VT​(tn,xn)≤VT​(t,x)+ε.\\limsup\_{n\\to\\infty}V\_{T}(t\_{n},x\_{n})\\leq V\_{T}(t,x)+\\varepsilon. |  |\
\
Since ε>0\\varepsilon>0 was arbitrary, VTV\_{T} is upper semicontinuous. Since gg is continuous, it holds that VT−gV\_{T}-g is upper semicontinuous and thus 𝒞T\\mathcal{C}\_{T} is open and 𝒟T\\mathcal{D}\_{T} is closed, with respect to 𝒮T\\mathcal{S}\_{T}.\
\
The second claim follows from standard optimal stopping arguments, see \[ [28](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib28 ""), Corollary 2.9\], if τT∗​(t,x)<∞\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x)<\\infty almost surely, which holds in the finite-horizon problem.\
∎\
\
If ∫0∞c⁡(t)​𝑑t<∞\\int\_{0}^{\\infty}c(t)\\,dt<\\infty, then stopping at infinity may also be optimal for the infinite-horizon problem. We later show that τT∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x) remains optimal in the infinite-horizon problem.\
\
Next, we analyze the structure of the state space.\
Introduce the set\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒰:={(t,x)∈𝒮:ℒ​g​(x)<−c⁡(t)}.\\mathcal{U}:=\\{(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S}:\\mathcal{L}g(x)<-c(t)\\}. |  |\
\
###### Proposition 3.8.\
\
Under Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), it holds that 𝒰∩𝒮T⊆𝒞T\\mathcal{U}\\cap\\mathcal{S}\_{T}\\subseteq\\mathcal{C}\_{T}.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Take any (t,x)∈𝒰∩𝒮T(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{U}\\cap\\mathcal{S}\_{T}. Since ℒ​g\\mathcal{L}g and cc are continuous, 𝒰\\mathcal{U} is open and thus 𝒰∩𝒮T\\mathcal{U}\\cap\\mathcal{S}\_{T} is open. Thus, there exists ε>0\\varepsilon>0 such that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Rε(t,x):=\[t,t+ε)×(x−ε,x+ε)⊂𝒰∩𝒮T.R\_{\\varepsilon}(t,x):=\[t,t+\\varepsilon)\\times(x-\\varepsilon,x+\\varepsilon)\\subset\\mathcal{U}\\cap\\mathcal{S}\_{T}. |  |\
\
Let\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | τε:=inf{u≥0:(t+u,Xux)∉Rε​(t,x)}\\tau\_{\\varepsilon}:=\\inf\\{u\\geq 0:(t+u,X\_{u}^{x})\\notin R\_{\\varepsilon}(t,x)\\} |  |\
\
be the first exit time from Rε​(t,x)R\_{\\varepsilon}(t,x), which we note is admissible even if T<∞T<\\infty. Furthermore, τε>0\\tau\_{\\varepsilon}>0 almost surely. Then\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | WT​(t,x)≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τε(c⁡(t+u)+ℒ​g​(Xux))​𝑑u\]<0.W\_{T}(t,x)\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau\_{\\varepsilon}}\\big(c(t+u)+\\mathcal{L}g(X\_{u}^{x})\\big)\\,du\\right\]<0. |  |\
\
Therefore VT​(t,x)<g⁡(x)V\_{T}(t,x)<g(x), and hence (t,x)∈𝒞T(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{C}\_{T}.\
∎\
\
Assumption [A.(v)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i5 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") guarantees a nonempty continuation region ex ante, since it implies that the tt–slice of 𝒰\\mathcal{U} is nonempty for all t≥0t\\geq 0. Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") then gives the basic interval structure of the tt–slice.\
\
###### Lemma 3.9.\
\
Under Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") it holds that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ℒ​g​(0+)=ℒ​g​(1−)=0.\\mathcal{L}g(0+)=\\mathcal{L}g(1-)=0. |  |\
\
Consequently, 𝒰⁡(t)⊂(0,1)\\mathcal{U}(t)\\subset(0,1) and 𝒰⁡(t)\\mathcal{U}(t) is an interval for every t≥0t\\geq 0, where 𝒰⁡(t):={x:(t,x)∈𝒰}\\mathcal{U}(t):=\\{x:(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{U}\\}.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
We only treat the case x→1x\\to 1 since the other endpoint is analogous. By Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), the limit ℒ​g​(1−)\\mathcal{L}g(1-) exists. Since gg is concave, ℒ​g≤0\\mathcal{L}g\\leq 0. Suppose, for contradiction, that ℒ​g​(1−)≤−ε\\mathcal{L}g(1-)\\leq-\\varepsilon for some ε>0\\varepsilon>0. By monotonicity we then have ℒ​g​(x)≤−ε\\mathcal{L}g(x)\\leq-\\varepsilon for all x∈(x0,1)x\\in(x\_{0},1), so that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | g′′​(x)≤−2​εσ2​(x),x∈(x0,1).g^{\\prime\\prime}(x)\\leq-\\frac{2\\varepsilon}{\\sigma^{2}(x)},\\qquad x\\in(x\_{0},1). |  |\
\
Integrating twice yields\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
|  | g⁡(x)\\displaystyle g(x) | =g⁡(x0)+∫x0x(g′​(x0)+∫x0yg′′​(z)​𝑑z)​𝑑y\\displaystyle=g(x\_{0})+\\int\_{x\_{0}}^{x}\\left(g^{\\prime}(x\_{0})+\\int\_{x\_{0}}^{y}g^{\\prime\\prime}(z)\\,dz\\right)\\,dy |  |\
|  |  | ≤g⁡(x0)+(x−x0)​g′​(x0)−2​ε​∫x0x∫x0y1σ2​(z)​𝑑z​𝑑y.\\displaystyle\\leq g(x\_{0})+(x-x\_{0})g^{\\prime}(x\_{0})-2\\varepsilon\\int\_{x\_{0}}^{x}\\int\_{x\_{0}}^{y}\\frac{1}{\\sigma^{2}(z)}\\,dz\\,dy. |  |\
\
Letting x→1x\\to 1 and using the Feller condition ( [3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E4 "In A.(i) ‣ Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) gives 0=g⁡(1)≤−∞0=g(1)\\leq-\\infty, a contradiction.\
\
For the second part, Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") implies that for each t≥0t\\geq 0, the equation\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ℒ​g​(x)=−c⁡(t)\\mathcal{L}g(x)=-c(t) |  |\
\
has at most two solutions, which lie inside (0,1)(0,1). Hence 𝒰⁡(t)\\mathcal{U}(t) is either empty or an open interval strictly contained in (0,1)(0,1). By Assumption [A.(v)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i5 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), 𝒰⁡(t)\\mathcal{U}(t) is nonempty and so we are done.\
∎\
\
We denote the upper boundary of 𝒰\\mathcal{U} by γ\\gamma, that is,\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | γ⁡(t):=sup{x∈(x0,1):ℒ​g​(x)+c⁡(t)=0}.\\gamma(t):=\\sup\\{x\\in(x\_{0},1):\\mathcal{L}g(x)+c(t)=0\\}. |  |\
\
By Lemma [3.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem9 "Lemma 3.9. ‣ 3.5. Preliminary Analysis ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), we have γ⁡(t)∈(x0,1)\\gamma(t)\\in(x\_{0},1) for all t≥0t\\geq 0. Furthermore, under the extended Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), it holds that (t,x)↦ℒ​g​(x)+c⁡(t)(t,x)\\mapsto\\mathcal{L}g(x)+c(t) is C1​(\[0,∞)×(0,1))C^{1}(\[0,\\infty)\\times(0,1)), and so the implicit function theorem yields that t↦γ⁡(t)t\\mapsto\\gamma(t) is C1​(\[0,∞))C^{1}(\[0,\\infty)) since Assumption [B.(ii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i2 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") ensures\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ∂x(ℒ​g​(x)+c⁡(t))\|x=γ⁡(t)>0,\\partial\_{x}(\\mathcal{L}g(x)+c(t))\\Big\|\_{x=\\gamma(t)}>0, |  |\
\
for t≥0t\\geq 0.\
\
## 4\. Main Results\
\
We now state the main results of the paper.\
The proofs are postponed to the following sections and will generally be proved first for the homogeneous infinite-horizon problem, then for the inhomogeneous finite-horizon, and finally for the inhomogeneous infinite-horizon problem. In all statements of the current section we assume Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and T≤∞T\\leq\\infty.\
The first main structural result shows that the continuation set inherits the same one-interval geometry as 𝒰\\mathcal{U}, namely, by Assumption [B.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i4 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"),\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒰={(t,x)∈𝒮:1−γ⁡(t)<x<γ⁡(t)}.\\mathcal{U}=\\{(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S}:1-\\gamma(t)<x<\\gamma(t)\\}. |  |\
\
###### Theorem 4.1(Structure of the continuation region).\
\
There exists a lower semicontinuous boundary bT:\[0,T)→(1/2,1)b\_{T}:\[0,T)\\to(1/2,1)\
such that\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (4.1) |  | 𝒞T={(t,x)∈𝒮T:1−bT​(t)<x<bT​(t)}.\\mathcal{C}\_{T}=\\{(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S}\_{T}:1-b\_{T}(t)<x<b\_{T}(t)\\}. |  |\
\
The first hitting time in ( [3.16](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E16 "In Proposition 3.7. ‣ 3.5. Preliminary Analysis ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) is given by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | τT∗​(t,x)=inf{0≤u<T−t:Xux∉(1−bT​(t+u),bT​(t+u))}∧(T−t)\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x)=\\inf\\{0\\leq u<T-t:X\_{u}^{x}\\not\\in(1-b\_{T}(t+u),b\_{T}(t+u))\\}\\wedge(T-t) |  |\
\
and is an optimal stopping time for ( [3.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E15 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) if T<∞T<\\infty and ( [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E2 "In 3.1.
