Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) if T=∞T=\\infty.\
Furthermore,\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | γ⁡(t)<bT​(t)<1,t<T.\\gamma(t)<b\_{T}(t)<1,\\qquad t<T. |  |\
\
For the inhomogeneous case, we establish Theorem [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1 (Structure of the continuation region). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") in four steps. First, we extend the conclusion of Proposition [3.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Proposition 3.7. ‣ 3.5. Preliminary Analysis ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") to the infinite-horizon problem by a limiting argument.\
Second, we prove existence of a boundary function satisfying\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | γ⁡(t)≤bT​(t)≤1,t<T.\\gamma(t)\\leq b\_{T}(t)\\leq 1,\\qquad t<T. |  |\
\
Third, we prove bT​(t)<1b\_{T}(t)<1. Finally, using the smooth fit obtained in Theorem [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Regularity and smooth fit). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") below, we prove bT​(t)>γ​(t)b\_{T}(t)>\\gamma(t).\
This result is the main qualitative description of the optimal rule.\
The decision maker continues only while the posterior remains sufficiently uncertain.\
The boundary bT​(t)b\_{T}(t) determines how close the posterior must be to certainty before stopping becomes optimal. In particular, the strict upper boundary bT​(t)<1b\_{T}(t)<1 for t<Tt<T implies that there always is a level of uncertainty at which it is optimal to make the decision.\
\
The next result records the regularity of the value function and establishes the well-known smooth-fit principle.\
\
###### Theorem 4.2(Regularity and smooth fit).\
\
The value function is VT∈C1,2​(𝒮T∖∂𝒟T)∩C1​(𝒮T)V\_{T}\\in C^{1,2}(\\mathcal{S}\_{T}\\setminus\\partial\\mathcal{D}\_{T})\\cap C^{1}(\\mathcal{S}\_{T}).\
\
The proof of this theorem is one of the main technical parts of the paper.\
For finite horizons, one can show probabilistic regularity of the optimal stopping boundary by showing that the lower semicontinuity of bTb\_{T} can be extended to Lipschitz continuity. This yields the spatial and temporal smooth fit. However, the arguments do not extend directly to the infinite-horizon case, due to integrability issues on an infinite horizon. Instead, we show the spatial smooth fit by a simple argument based on uniform convergence.\
\
We can also show that the regularity found in Theorem [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Regularity and smooth fit). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") is in some sense maximal.\
\
###### Corollary 4.3.\
\
The second spatial derivative of the value function admits the representation\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ∂x​xVT​(t,x)={−2​c⁡(t)+∂tVT​(t,x)σ2​(x)for ​(t,x)∈𝒞Tg′′​(x)for ​(t,x)∈(𝒟T)∘.\\partial\_{xx}V\_{T}(t,x)=\\begin{cases}-2\\frac{c(t)+\\partial\_{t}V\_{T}(t,x)}{\\sigma^{2}(x)}&\\text{for }(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{C}\_{T}\\\<br>g^{\\prime\\prime}(x)&\\text{for }(t,x)\\in(\\mathcal{D}\_{T})^{\\circ}.\\end{cases} |  |\
\
Specifically, it is not continuous across the boundary.\
\
The specific representation of the temporal derivative will be given in ( [6.23](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E23 "In Theorem 6.10. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) and ( [6.14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E14 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) below for finite and infinite horizons, respectively.\
The smooth-fit principle also enables us to upgrade the Lipschitz continuity of bTb\_{T} to C1​(\[0,T))C^{1}(\[0,T)) by direct application of \[ [10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib10 "")\].\
\
###### Corollary 4.4.\
\
The boundary satisfies bT∈C1​(\[0,T))b\_{T}\\in C^{1}(\[0,T)).\
\
The regularity in Theorem [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Regularity and smooth fit). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") leads to an equation for the unknown boundary itself.\
The integral equation is particularly useful for numerical schemes based on Picard iteration.\
\
###### Theorem 4.5(Free-boundary integral equation).\
\
The optimal boundary bTb\_{T} solves\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (4.2) |  | g(x)=𝔼\[g(XxT−t)\\displaystyle g(x)=\\mathbb{E}\\Bigg\[g(X^{x}\_{T-t}) | +∫0T−tc(t+u)𝟏{Xxu∈(1−bT(t+u),bT(t+u))}du\\displaystyle+\\int\_{0}^{T-t}c(t+u)\\mathbf{1}\_{\\{X^{x}\_{u}\\in(1-b\_{T}(t+u),b\_{T}(t+u))\\}}\\,du |  |\
|  |  | −∫0T−tℒg(Xxu)𝟏{Xxu∉(1−bT(t+u),bT(t+u))}du\],\\displaystyle-\\int\_{0}^{T-t}\\mathcal{L}g(X^{x}\_{u})\\mathbf{1}\_{\\{X^{x}\_{u}\\not\\in(1-b\_{T}(t+u),b\_{T}(t+u))\\}}\\,du\\Bigg\], |  |\
\
for x=bT​(t)x=b\_{T}(t) and x=1−bT​(t)x=1-b\_{T}(t) with t<Tt<T. For T=∞T=\\infty, the integral is interpreted as the monotone limit as the integration domain expands and g⁡(XT−tx)=0g(X^{x}\_{T-t})=0 since X∞x∈{0,1}X^{x}\_{\\infty}\\in\\{0,1\\}, almost surely.\
\
Moreover, this equation characterizes the optimal boundary uniquely in the following sense.\
If β:\[0,T)→(1/2,1)\\beta:\[0,T)\\to(1/2,1) is a continuous function satisfying\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | γ⁡(t)<β⁡(t)<1\\gamma(t)<\\beta(t)<1 |  |\
\
and solves ( [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.E2 "In Theorem 4.5 (Free-boundary integral equation). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) with bT​(t)b\_{T}(t) replaced by β⁡(t)\\beta(t) for all t<Tt<T, then β​(t)=bT​(t)\\beta(t)=b\_{T}(t) for all t<Tt<T.\
\
Before proving these statements under Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), we will first take a detour into the homogeneous infinite-horizon problem, which admits constant optimal stopping boundaries, when they exist. The proof techniques are different and allow for less regularity in the assumptions.\
\
## 5\. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems\
\
In this section we assume Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") with T=∞T=\\infty and the time cost cc being constant.\
Then the infinite-horizon optimal stopping problem, ( [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E2 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), becomes time-homogeneous. That is, we are to solve\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (5.1) |  | V⁡(x)=infτ∈𝒯𝔼⁡\[∫0τc​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\],V(x)=\\inf\_{\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c\\,du+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\\right\], |  |\
\
for a process XxX^{x} given by ( [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E1 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). We note that τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T} is equivalent to 𝔼⁡\[τ\]<∞\\mathbb{E}\[\\tau\]<\\infty for such a constant time cost. As usual in homogeneous one-dimensional optimal stopping problems, we formulate a free-boundary problem and verify that it indeed solves the optimal stopping problem. The natural free-boundary problem is as follows:\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (5.2) |  | ℒ​V^​(x)\\displaystyle\\mathcal{L}\\widehat{V}(x) | =−c\\displaystyle=-c |  | x∈(A,B)\\displaystyle x\\in(A,B) |  |\
| (5.3) |  | V^​(x)\\displaystyle\\widehat{V}(x) | =g⁡(x)\\displaystyle=g(x) |  | x∈{A,B}\\displaystyle x\\in\\{A,B\\} |  |\
| (5.4) |  | V^′​(x)\\displaystyle\\widehat{V}^{\\prime}(x) | =g′​(x)\\displaystyle=g^{\\prime}(x) |  | x∈{A,B}\\displaystyle x\\in\\{A,B\\} |  |\
| (5.5) |  | V^​(x)\\displaystyle\\widehat{V}(x) | <g⁡(x)\\displaystyle<g(x) |  | x∈(A,B)\\displaystyle x\\in(A,B) |  |\
| (5.6) |  | V^​(x)\\displaystyle\\widehat{V}(x) | =g⁡(x)\\displaystyle=g(x) |  | x∈\[0,1\]∖(A,B)\\displaystyle x\\in\[0,1\]\\setminus(A,B) |  |\
| (5.7) |  | 0<A\\displaystyle 0<A | <B<1.\\displaystyle<B<1. |  |\
\
Note that since we only work under Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), we have formulated the problem to allow for asymmetric boundaries.\
The following is an extension of \[ [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib9 "")\] for general win-martingales.\
\
###### Theorem 5.1.\
\
Under Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), the free-boundary problem admits a solution V^∈C2​((0,1)∖{A,B})∩C1​((0,1))\\widehat{V}\\in C^{2}((0,1)\\setminus\\{A,B\\})\\cap C^{1}((0,1)) which is unique. If Assumptions [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")- [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and ℒ​g​(x0)≥−c\\mathcal{L}g(x\_{0})\\geq-c hold, then ( [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E2 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"))–( [5.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E6 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) can only hold if A=BA=B, that is, V^=g\\widehat{V}=g.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
We rewrite the ODE in ( [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E2 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) as\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (5.8) |  | V^′′​(x)=−2​cσ2​(x).\\widehat{V}^{\\prime\\prime}(x)=-\\frac{2c}{\\sigma^{2}(x)}. |  |\
\
Let ψ\\psi be any particular solution to the inhomogeneous ODE ( [5.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E8 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Then the general solution is ψ⁡(x)+c1​x+c2\\psi(x)+c\_{1}x+c\_{2} for constants c1c\_{1} and c2c\_{2}. Using the boundary conditions for smooth and continuous fit, ( [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E3 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) and ( [5.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E4 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), at AA, we get the representation for the candidate solution\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (5.9) |  | V^A​(x):=(x−A)​(g′​(A)−ψ′​(A))+g⁡(A)−ψ⁡(A)+ψ⁡(x)\\widehat{V}\_{A}(x):=(x-A)(g^{\\prime}(A)-\\psi^{\\prime}(A))+g(A)-\\psi(A)+\\psi(x) |  |\
\
for x∈(A,B)x\\in(A,B).\
\
Define W^A​(x)=V^A​(x)−g⁡(x)\\widehat{W}\_{A}(x)=\\widehat{V}\_{A}(x)-g(x) and H⁡(x)=g⁡(x)−ψ⁡(x)H(x)=g(x)-\\psi(x). We can then write\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W^A​(x)=(x−A)​H′​(A)+H⁡(A)−H⁡(x).\\widehat{W}\_{A}(x)=(x-A)H^{\\prime}(A)+H(A)-H(x). |  |\
\
The smooth and continuous fit at BB can be written in terms of HH as\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (5.10) |  | H′​(A)\\displaystyle H^{\\prime}(A) | =H′​(B)\\displaystyle=H^{\\prime}(B) |  |\
| (5.11) |  | H′​(B)\\displaystyle H^{\\prime}(B) | =H⁡(B)−H⁡(A)B−A\\displaystyle=\\frac{H(B)-H(A)}{B-A} |  |\
\
given A≠BA\\neq B, as will be shown later. That is, we want to show that there exists a unique pair of points such that the tangents of HH at these points are equal and the slope of the secant line connecting the function values coincides with the tangents. To this end, we will need to inspect the HH function and thus the gg and ψ\\psi functions.\
\
By Lemma [3.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem9 "Lemma 3.9. ‣ 3.5. Preliminary Analysis ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), we have ℒ​g​(0+)=ℒ​g​(1−)=0\\mathcal{L}g(0+)=\\mathcal{L}g(1-)=0 and the equation ℒ​g​(x)=−c\\mathcal{L}g(x)=-c has two unique solutions 0<x∗<x∗<10<x\_{\*}<x^{\*}<1. Thus,\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | H′′​(x)=g′′​(x)−ψ′′​(x)=2σ2​(x)​(ℒ​g​(x)+c),H^{\\prime\\prime}(x)=g^{\\prime\\prime}(x)-\\psi^{\\prime\\prime}(x)=\\frac{2}{\\sigma^{2}(x)}\\left(\\mathcal{L}g(x)+c\\right), |  |\
\
which leads us to conclude H′H^{\\prime} is strictly increasing on (0,x∗)(0,x\_{\*}) and (x∗,1)(x^{\*},1) while strictly decreasing on (x∗,x∗)(x\_{\*},x^{\*}). Furthermore, for any fixed y∈(0,1)y\\in(0,1), we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ψ′(x)=∫yxψ′′(z)dz+ψ′(y)=−∫yx2​cσ2​(z)dz+ψ′(y),\\psi^{\\prime}(x)=\\int\_{y}^{x}\\psi^{\\prime\\prime}(z)dz+\\psi^{\\prime}(y)=-\\int\_{y}^{x}\\frac{2c}{\\sigma^{2}(z)}dz+\\psi^{\\prime}(y), |  |\
\
which diverges by ( [3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E4 "In A.(i) ‣ Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) since for x>yx>y we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ∫yx1σ2​(z)​𝑑z≥∫yxx−zσ2​(z)​𝑑z=∫yx∫zxd​u​1σ2​(z)​𝑑z=∫yx∫yu1σ2​(z)​𝑑z​𝑑u→∞\\int\_{y}^{x}\\frac{1}{\\sigma^{2}(z)}\\,dz\\geq\\int\_{y}^{x}\\frac{x-z}{\\sigma^{2}(z)}\\,dz=\\int\_{y}^{x}\\int\_{z}^{x}\\,du\\,\\frac{1}{\\sigma^{2}(z)}\\,dz=\\int\_{y}^{x}\\int\_{y}^{u}\\frac{1}{\\sigma^{2}(z)}\\,dz\\,du\\to\\infty |  |\
\
for x→1x\\to 1 by Fubini’s theorem. Hence, we have ψ′​(1−)=−∞\\psi^{\\prime}(1-)=-\\infty and a similar argument shows ψ′​(0+)=∞\\psi^{\\prime}(0+)=\\infty. As for\
H′H^{\\prime} we can write\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | H′​(x)=g′​(x)−ψ′​(x)=ψ′​(x)​(g′​(x)ψ′​(x)−1)H^{\\prime}(x)=g^{\\prime}(x)-\\psi^{\\prime}(x)=\\psi^{\\prime}(x)\\left(\\frac{g^{\\prime}(x)}{\\psi^{\\prime}(x)}-1\\right) |  |\
\
and we want to show H′​(0+)=−∞H^{\\prime}(0+)=-\\infty and H′​(1−)=∞H^{\\prime}(1-)=\\infty. We only consider the x→0x\\to 0 case, the case x→1x\\to 1 being similar. Since gg is concave with g⁡(0)=0g(0)=0 and g⁡(x)>0g(x)>0 for x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1), g′​(x)>0g^{\\prime}(x)>0 for sufficiently small x>0x>0, and limx↘0g′​(x)\\lim\_{x\\searrow 0}g^{\\prime}(x) exists. Suppose limx↘0g′​(x)<∞\\lim\_{x\\searrow 0}g^{\\prime}(x)<\\infty. Then H′​(0+)=−∞H^{\\prime}(0+)=-\\infty follows from ψ′​(x)→∞\\psi^{\\prime}(x)\\to\\infty. Conversely, if limx↘0g′​(x)=∞\\lim\_{x\\searrow 0}g^{\\prime}(x)=\\infty, we consider\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | g′′​(x)ψ′′​(x)=ℒ​g​(x)−c→0\\frac{g^{\\prime\\prime}(x)}{\\psi^{\\prime\\prime}(x)}=\\frac{\\mathcal{L}g(x)}{-c}\\to 0 |  |\
\
for x→0x\\to 0 since ℒ​g​(0+)=0\\mathcal{L}g(0+)=0, which eventually converges monotonically by Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). Thus, by L’Hôpital’s rule, (g′​(x)ψ′​(x)−1)→−1\\left(\\frac{g^{\\prime}(x)}{\\psi^{\\prime}(x)}-1\\right)\\to-1 eventually bounded away from 0 and thus, H′​(0+)=−∞H^{\\prime}(0+)=-\\infty and similarly H′​(1−)=∞H^{\\prime}(1-)=\\infty.\
\
By the preceding facts we get that there exists a unique solution x¯∈(0,x∗)\\underline{x}\\in(0,x\_{\*}) to the equation H′​(x¯)=H′​(x∗)H^{\\prime}(\\underline{x})=H^{\\prime}(x^{\*}), and likewise a unique x¯∈(x∗,1)\\overline{x}\\in(x^{\*},1) such that H′​(x¯)=H′​(x∗)H^{\\prime}(\\overline{x})=H^{\\prime}(x\_{\*}). For x~∈(x∗,x∗)\\tilde{x}\\in(x\_{\*},x^{\*}) there exist two solutions to H′​(x)=H′​(x~)H^{\\prime}(x)=H^{\\prime}(\\tilde{x}), one in (x¯,x∗)(\\underline{x},x\_{\*}) and one in (x∗,x¯)(x^{\*},\\overline{x}). Finally, a solution to ( [5.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E10 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) cannot exist in \[0,x¯)∪(x¯,1\]\[0,\\underline{x})\\cup(\\overline{x},1\]. We note that the solution to ( [5.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E10 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) must have A∈\[x¯,x∗\]A\\in\[\\underline{x},x\_{\*}\] and B∈\[x∗,x¯\]B\\in\[x^{\*},\\overline{x}\] since if, say, A∈(x∗,x∗)A\\in(x\_{\*},x^{\*}) then\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W^A​(x)=(x−A)​H′​(A)+H⁡(A)−H⁡(x)=∫AxH′​(A)−H′​(y)​𝑑y>0\\widehat{W}\_{A}(x)=(x-A)H^{\\prime}(A)+H(A)-H(x)=\\int\_{A}^{x}H^{\\prime}(A)-H^{\\prime}(y)\\,dy>0 |  |\
\
for x∈(A,x∗)x\\in(A,x^{\*}), which contradicts V^A​(x)≤g​(x)\\widehat{V}\_{A}(x)\\leq g(x). A similar argument holds for BB. In particular we must have A<BA<B.\
\
To summarize our findings, we have that A∈\[x¯,x∗\]A\\in\[\\underline{x},x\_{\*}\] and B∈\[x∗,x¯\]B\\in\[x^{\*},\\overline{x}\] which are disjoint sets.\
Defining the restrictions H¯:=H\|\[x¯,x∗\]\\underline{H}:=H\|\_{\[\\underline{x},x\_{\*}\]} and H¯:=H\|\[x∗,x¯\]\\overline{H}:=H\|\_{\[x^{\*},\\overline{x}\]} we have two continuous convex functions on disjoint compact domains.\
Thus, by Bisztriczky’s theorem, see \[ [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib9 "")\], there exists exactly one common supporting tangent line ℓ⁡(x):=s​x+r\\ell(x):=sx+r where\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | s=H⁡(B)−H⁡(A)B−A.s=\\frac{H(B)-H(A)}{B-A}. |  |\
\
A sufficient condition for this tangent line of the restricted functions to be a tangent of the function HH, and hence be equal to the slope of HH at AA and BB, is that A∈(x¯,x∗)A\\in(\\underline{x},x\_{\*}) and B∈(x∗,x¯)B\\in(x^{\*},\\overline{x}).\
Assume for contradiction that ℓ\\ell supports H¯\\underline{H} at x¯\\underline{x}. Then\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (5.12) |  | s≤H′​(x¯),s\\leq H^{\\prime}(\\underline{x}), |  |\
\
by convexity.\
If ℓ\\ell supports H¯\\overline{H} at B∈(x∗,x¯)B\\in(x^{\*},\\overline{x}), then\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | s=H′​(B)>H′​(x∗)=H′​(x¯),s=H^{\\prime}(B)>H^{\\prime}(x^{\*})=H^{\\prime}(\\underline{x}), |  |\
\
and if ℓ\\ell supports H¯\\overline{H} at B=x¯B=\\overline{x}, then\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | s≥H′​(x¯)>H′​(x¯),s\\geq H^{\\prime}(\\overline{x})>H^{\\prime}(\\underline{x}), |  |\
\
and both cases lead to a contradiction. Hence ℓ\\ell must support H¯\\overline{H} at x∗x^{\*}. However, this yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | s=H⁡(x∗)−H⁡(x¯)x∗−x¯=1x∗−x¯​∫x¯x∗H′​(y)​𝑑y>1x∗−x¯​∫x¯x∗H′​(x¯)​𝑑y=H′​(x¯),s=\\frac{H(x^{\*})-H(\\underline{x})}{x^{\*}-\\underline{x}}=\\frac{1}{x^{\*}-\\underline{x}}\\int\_{\\underline{x}}^{x^{\*}}H^{\\prime}(y)\\,dy>\\frac{1}{x^{\*}-\\underline{x}}\\int\_{\\underline{x}}^{x^{\*}}H^{\\prime}(\\underline{x})\\,dy=H^{\\prime}(\\underline{x}), |  |\
\
which also contradicts ( [5.12](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E12 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Analogous arguments rule out support at the endpoints x∗,x∗x\_{\*},x^{\*}, and x¯\\overline{x}. Hence there exists a unique solution to ( [5.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E10 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) and ( [5.11](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E11 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) with A∈(x¯,x∗)A\\in(\\underline{x},x\_{\*}) and B∈(x∗,x¯)B\\in(x^{\*},\\overline{x}).\
\
Finally, we can also check the inequality constraint ( [5.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E5 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Recall H′​(A)−H′​(x)H^{\\prime}(A)-H^{\\prime}(x) has roots at AA and BB, and some point x~∈(x∗,x∗)\\tilde{x}\\in(x\_{\*},x^{\*}). Furthermore, it is strictly negative for x∈(A,x~)x\\in(A,\\tilde{x}) and strictly positive for x∈(x~,B)x\\in(\\tilde{x},B). Hence,\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W^A​(x)=∫AxH′​(A)−H′​(y)​𝑑y<0\\widehat{W}\_{A}(x)=\\int\_{A}^{x}H^{\\prime}(A)-H^{\\prime}(y)\\,dy<0 |  |\
\
for x∈(A,x~\]x\\in(A,\\tilde{x}\], and similarly, for x∈\[x~,B)x\\in\[\\tilde{x},B)\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W^A(x)=W^A(x)−W^A(B)=−∫xBH′(A)−H′(y)dy<0.\\widehat{W}\_{A}(x)=\\widehat{W}\_{A}(x)-\\widehat{W}\_{A}(B)=-\\int\_{x}^{B}H^{\\prime}(A)-H^{\\prime}(y)\\,dy<0. |  |\
\
By pasting to obey ( [5.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E6 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we conclude V^A​(⋅)\\widehat{V}\_{A}(\\cdot) solves the free-boundary problem uniquely, and hence we are done with the first claim.\
\
For the latter claim, assume ℒ​g​(x0)≥−c\\mathcal{L}g(x\_{0})\\geq-c and ( [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E2 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"))–( [5.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E7 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Then, by Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and the ODE ( [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E2 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we have ℒ​g​(x)≥−c=ℒ​V^A​(x)\\mathcal{L}g(x)\\geq-c=\\mathcal{L}\\widehat{V}\_{A}(x) for x∈(A,B)x\\in(A,B). Specifically, Assumption [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") yields that the inequality is strict for all x∈(A,B)∖{x0}x\\in(A,B)\\setminus\\{x\_{0}\\}, that is, we have g′′​(x)>V^A′′​(x)g^{\\prime\\prime}(x)>\\widehat{V}\_{A}^{\\prime\\prime}(x) for a set with positive measure. Thus,\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V^A​(B)\\displaystyle\\widehat{V}\_{A}(B) | =V^A​(A)+∫AB(V^A′​(A)+∫AyV^A′′​(z)​𝑑z)​𝑑y\\displaystyle=\\widehat{V}\_{A}(A)+\\int\_{A}^{B}\\left(\\widehat{V}\_{A}^{\\prime}(A)+\\int\_{A}^{y}\\widehat{V}\_{A}^{\\prime\\prime}(z)\\,dz\\right)\\,dy |  |\
|  |  | <g⁡(A)+∫AB(g′​(A)+∫Ayg′′​(z)​𝑑z)​𝑑y=g⁡(B),\\displaystyle<g(A)+\\int\_{A}^{B}\\left(g^{\\prime}(A)+\\int\_{A}^{y}g^{\\prime\\prime}(z)\\,dz\\right)\\,dy=g(B), |  |\
\
and hence ( [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E3 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) cannot hold at BB, so we reach a contradiction.\
∎\
\
We can now verify that the solution to the free-boundary problem solves the optimal stopping problem. The verification follows from the generalized Itô formula and a standard martingale argument, see e.g. \[ [28](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib28 "")\]. We provide the main steps below.\
\
###### Theorem 5.2.\
\
The following holds for the optimal stopping problem in ( [5.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E1 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")):\
\
1. (i)\
\
\
If Assumptions [A.(i)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i1 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")- [A.(iv)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i4 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and ℒ​g​(x0)≥−c\\mathcal{L}g(x\_{0})\\geq-c hold, then V≡gV\\equiv g.\
\
2. (ii)\
\
\
Under Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), the optimal solution is given by\
\
\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V⁡(x)={V^A∗​(x),x∈(A∗,B∗)g⁡(x),x∈\[0,A∗\]∪\[B∗,1\],V(x)=\\begin{cases}\\widehat{V}\_{A^{\*}}(x),&x\\in(A^{\*},B^{\*})\\\<br>g(x),&x\\in\[0,A^{\*}\]\\cup\[B^{\*},1\],\\end{cases} |  |\
\
\
\
where V^A∗​(x)\\widehat{V}\_{A^{\*}}(x) is given by ( [5.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E9 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), and A∗A^{\*} and B∗B^{\*} form the unique solution pair to the equations ( [5.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E10 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) and ( [5.11](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E11 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
\
\
###### Proof.\
\
For the case (i)(i), we get ℒ​g​(x)≥−c\\mathcal{L}g(x)\\geq-c for all xx. Since c+ℒ​g≥0c+\\mathcal{L}g\\geq 0, the Lagrange formulation gives W≥0W\\geq 0. Immediate stopping gives W≤0W\\leq 0, hence W=0W=0 and V=gV=g.\
For the case (i​i)(ii), instead of working with VV, we will verify using the Lagrange formulation of the problem. Let\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W⁡(x):=V⁡(x)−g⁡(x)=infτ𝔼⁡\[∫0τ(c+ℒ​g​(Xux))​𝑑u\],W(x):=V(x)-g(x)=\\inf\_{\\tau}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}\\left(c+\\mathcal{L}g(X^{x}\_{u})\\right)\\,du\\right\], |  |\
\
where the equality follows from ( [3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E8 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). The candidate solution is WA∗​(x)=V^A∗​(x)−g⁡(x)W\_{A^{\*}}(x)=\\widehat{V}\_{A^{\*}}(x)-g(x) for x∈(A∗,B∗)x\\in(A^{\*},B^{\*}) and WA∗​(x)=0W\_{A^{\*}}(x)=0 otherwise.\
By the change-of-variable formula with local time on curves \[ [27](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib27 "")\], and by the smooth fit, we have\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (5.13) |  | WA∗(Xtx)=WA∗(x)+∫0tℒWA∗(Xsx)𝟏{Xxs∉{A∗,B∗}}ds+∫0tσ(Xsx)WA∗′(Xsx)dBs.W\_{A^{\*}}(X^{x}\_{t})=W\_{A^{\*}}(x)+\\int\_{0}^{t}\\mathcal{L}W\_{A^{\*}}(X^{x}\_{s})\\mathbf{1}\_{\\{X^{x}\_{s}\\not\\in\\{A^{\*},B^{\*}\\}\\}}\\,ds+\\int\_{0}^{t}\\sigma(X^{x}\_{s})W\_{A^{\*}}^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{s})dB\_{s}. |  |\
\
Note that σ⁡(x)​WA∗′​(x)\\sigma(x)W\_{A^{\*}}^{\\prime}(x) is globally bounded since A∗A^{\*} and B∗B^{\*} are bounded away from {0,1}\\{0,1\\}, so the local martingale term, Mt=∫0tσ⁡(Xsx)​WA∗′​(Xsx)​d​BsM\_{t}=\\int\_{0}^{t}\\sigma(X^{x}\_{s})W\_{A^{\*}}^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{s})dB\_{s}, is a square-integrable martingale. Since we consider stopping times with 𝔼⁡\[τ\]<∞\\mathbb{E}\[\\tau\]<\\infty, we get 𝔼⁡\[Mτ\]=0\\mathbb{E}\[M\_{\\tau}\]=0. Thus, by using ( [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E2 "In 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) in (A∗,B∗)(A^{\*},B^{\*}) and WA∗​(x)=0W\_{A^{\*}}(x)=0 together with c+ℒ​g​(x)≥0c+\\mathcal{L}g(x)\\geq 0 for x∉(A∗,B∗)x\\not\\in(A^{\*},B^{\*}) in ( [5.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E13 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0τ(c+ℒ​g​(Xux))​𝑑u\]≥WA∗​(x)\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}\\left(c+\\mathcal{L}g(X^{x}\_{u})\\right)\\,du\\right\]\\geq W\_{A^{\*}}(x) |  |\
\
for any τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T} by taking expectation. Taking infimum, we arrive at W⁡(x)≥WA∗​(x)W(x)\\geq W\_{A^{\*}}(x). Conversely, plugging τ∗​(x)=inf{t≥0:Xtx∉(A∗,B∗)}\\tau^{\*}(x)=\\inf\\{t\\geq 0:X^{x}\_{t}\\not\\in(A^{\*},B^{\*})\\}, which satisfies 𝔼​\[τ∗​(x)\]<∞\\mathbb{E}\[\\tau^{\*}(x)\]<\\infty, into ( [5.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.E13 "In Proof. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | WA∗​(x)=𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∗​(x)(c+ℒ​g​(Xux))​𝑑u\]≥W⁡(x),W\_{A^{\*}}(x)=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}(x)}\\left(c+\\mathcal{L}g(X^{x}\_{u})\\right)\\,du\\right\]\\geq W(x), |  |\
\
by taking expectation.\
∎\
\
Thus, one-dimensional asymmetric versions of Theorems [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1 (Structure of the continuation region). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Regularity and smooth fit). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") also hold under the baseline Assumption [A](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt1 "Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") given a constant running cost cc. Similarly, an asymmetric version of Corollary [4.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem4 "Corollary 4.4. ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") holds trivially while asymmetric versions of Corollary [4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem3 "Corollary 4.3. ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and Theorem [4.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 4.5 (Free-boundary integral equation). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") can be shown by similar arguments as for the inhomogeneous case below. The nonconstant running-cost case requires more work, but the homogeneous infinite-horizon problem will be useful for comparison in multiple results below.\
\
## 6\. Inhomogeneous Problems\
\
In this section we will work under the extended Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
We first tackle initial regularity results for the value function to obtain estimates on the derivatives. Then we turn to regularity of the optimal stopping boundary and finally we move on to the smooth fit and upgrade the regularity of the optimal stopping boundary.\
\
It should first be noted that many regularity results have a local nature which in the temporal coordinate is located in the open set (0,T)(0,T). To extend the result to hold on \[0,T)\[0,T) one may consider an extension of the running cost cc. Namely, it is not hard to see that for every ε>0\\varepsilon>0 there exists an extension c¯:\[0,∞)→(0,∞)\\bar{c}:\[0,\\infty)\\to(0,\\infty) such that c¯​(t+ε)=c​(t)\\bar{c}(t+\\varepsilon)=c(t) and the problem with the c¯\\bar{c} running cost satisfies Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). If VTV\_{T} denotes the original value function and V¯T+ε\\bar{V}\_{T+\\varepsilon} denotes the value function with the running cost c¯\\bar{c} on an extended horizon, then\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.1) |  | VT​(t,x)=V¯T+ε​(t+ε,x).V\_{T}(t,x)=\\bar{V}\_{T+\\varepsilon}(t+\\varepsilon,x). |  |\
\
This means that if a regularity property can be shown on (0,T+ε)(0,T+\\varepsilon) for general problems such as V¯T+ε\\bar{V}\_{T+\\varepsilon} then the property specifically holds on \[0,T)\[0,T) for VTV\_{T}.\
\
### 6.1. Properties of the value function\
\
We first show that the infinite-horizon problem can be realized as the limit of the finite-horizon problems, which in turn yields existence of an optimal stopping time in the infinite-horizon problem.\
\
###### Proposition 6.1.\
\
For every (t,x)∈𝒮(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S} it holds that VT​(t,x)↘V∞​(t,x)V\_{T}(t,x)\\searrow V\_{\\infty}(t,x) and τT∗​(t,x)↗τ∞∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x)\\nearrow\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x) as T↗∞T\\nearrow\\infty with T>tT>t. Furthermore, τ∞∗\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty} is an optimal stopping time for ( [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E2 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
\
###### Proof.\
\
It holds that T↦VT​(t,x)T\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is decreasing since the set of admissible stopping times is increasing and similarly VT​(t,x)≥V∞​(t,x)V\_{T}(t,x)\\geq V\_{\\infty}(t,x) for all T>tT>t. Furthermore, VT​(t,x)≥0V\_{T}(t,x)\\geq 0 so a limit exists, say V∞​(t,x)V^{\\infty}(t,x), thus V∞​(t,x)≥V∞​(t,x)V^{\\infty}(t,x)\\geq V\_{\\infty}(t,x). Now fix an ε>0\\varepsilon>0 and take τ∈𝒯\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T} such that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\]≤V∞​(t,x)+ε.\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\\right\]\\leq V\_{\\infty}(t,x)+\\varepsilon. |  |\
\
For each TT, τ∧(T−t)\\tau\\wedge(T-t) is admissible for the VTV\_{T} problem. Hence,\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | VT​(t,x)≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∧(T−t)c⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτ∧(T−t)x)\].V\_{T}(t,x)\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau\\wedge(T-t)}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau\\wedge(T-t)})\\right\]. |  |\
\
Since τ\\tau and τ∧(T−t)\\tau\\wedge(T-t) are in 𝒯\\mathcal{T}, monotone convergence yields the limit of the integral term, while dominated convergence yields the limit of the gg term by the path continuity of XxX^{x} and g⁡(Xτx)=0g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})=0 on the event {τ=∞}\\{\\tau=\\infty\\}. Hence, taking limsup, we get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V∞​(t,x)=lim supT→∞VT​(t,x)≤V∞​(t,x)+ε,V^{\\infty}(t,x)=\\limsup\_{T\\to\\infty}V\_{T}(t,x)\\leq V\_{\\infty}(t,x)+\\varepsilon, |  |\
\
and letting ε→0\\varepsilon\\to 0, we arrive at VT↘V∞V\_{T}\\searrow V\_{\\infty} pointwise. Define the extended stopping and continuation sets\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒟^T:=𝒟T∪(\[T,∞)×(0,1)),𝒞^T:=𝒮∖𝒟^T.\\hat{\\mathcal{D}}\_{T}:=\\mathcal{D}\_{T}\\cup(\[T,\\infty)\\times(0,1)),\\qquad\\hat{\\mathcal{C}}\_{T}:=\\mathcal{S}\\setminus\\hat{\\mathcal{D}}\_{T}. |  |\
\
From the monotonicity it is clear that a limit exists, 𝒟^T↘𝒟∞\\hat{\\mathcal{D}}\_{T}\\searrow\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}, and 𝒞^T↗𝒞∞\\hat{\\mathcal{C}}\_{T}\\nearrow\\mathcal{C}^{\\infty}. Furthermore, the limits are closed and open, respectively. The monotonicity also yields a limit τT∗​(t,x)↗τ∞​(t,x)\\tau\_{T}^{\*}(t,x)\\nearrow\\tau^{\\infty}(t,x) since\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
|  | T↦τT∗​(t,x)\\displaystyle T\\mapsto\\tau\_{T}^{\*}(t,x) | =inf{s≥0:(t+s,Xsx)∈𝒟T}∧(T−t)\\displaystyle=\\inf\\{s\\geq 0:(t+s,X^{x}\_{s})\\in\\mathcal{D}\_{T}\\}\\wedge(T-t) |  |\
|  |  | =inf{s≥0:(t+s,Xsx)∈𝒟^T}\\displaystyle=\\inf\\{s\\geq 0:(t+s,X^{x}\_{s})\\in\\widehat{\\mathcal{D}}\_{T}\\} |  |\
\
is increasing pathwise. It holds that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | τ∞​(t,x)=limT→∞τT∗​(t,x)≤τ𝒟∞​(t,x):=inf{s≥0:(t+s,Xsx)∈𝒟∞}.\\tau^{\\infty}(t,x)=\\lim\_{T\\to\\infty}\\tau\_{T}^{\*}(t,x)\\leq\\tau\_{\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}}(t,x):=\\inf\\{s\\geq 0:(t+s,X^{x}\_{s})\\in\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}\\}. |  |\
\
For the converse, we make the following pathwise argument. Take any r<τ𝒟∞r<\\tau\_{\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}}. Then\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Kr:={(t+s,Xsx):0≤s≤r}K\_{r}:=\\{(t+s,X^{x}\_{s}):0\\leq s\\leq r\\} |  |\
\
is a compact subset of 𝒞∞\\mathcal{C}^{\\infty} since Xsx∈(0,1)X^{x}\_{s}\\in(0,1) for finite times and the paths are continuous. Since 𝒞∞\\mathcal{C}^{\\infty} is a union of increasing open sets,\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝒞∞=⋃T>t𝒞^T,\\mathcal{C}^{\\infty}=\\bigcup\_{T>t}\\hat{\\mathcal{C}}\_{T}, |  |\
\
there exists some T0>t+rT\_{0}>t+r such that for all T>T0T>T\_{0} it holds that Kr⊆𝒞^TK\_{r}\\subseteq\\hat{\\mathcal{C}}\_{T}. From this, we conclude that τT∗​(t,x)>r\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x)>r for all T>T0T>T\_{0}. Since rr was arbitrary, we can arrive at\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | τ∞​(t,x)=limT↗∞τT∗​(t,x)≥τ𝒟∞​(t,x).\\tau^{\\infty}(t,x)=\\lim\_{T\\nearrow\\infty}\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x)\\geq\\tau\_{\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}}(t,x). |  |\
\
In total, we have τ∞=τ𝒟∞\\tau^{\\infty}=\\tau\_{\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}}.\
\
We need to identify the limit of the stopping times with the proposed optimal stopping time for the infinite-horizon problem given in ( [3.16](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E16 "In Proposition 3.7. ‣ 3.5. Preliminary Analysis ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). To this end, note that since V∞≤VTV\_{\\infty}\\leq V\_{T}, 𝒟⊆𝒟^T\\mathcal{D}\\subseteq\\widehat{\\mathcal{D}}\_{T} for all TT, and so 𝒟⊆𝒟∞\\mathcal{D}\\subseteq\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}. For the converse, if (t,x)∈𝒟∞(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty}, then (t,x)∈𝒟^T(t,x)\\in\\widehat{\\mathcal{D}}\_{T} for all T>tT>t, i.e. (t,x)∈𝒟T(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{D}\_{T}, thus VT​(t,x)=g​(x)V\_{T}(t,x)=g(x). But then, by the convergence of the value function, g⁡(x)=VT​(t,x)→V∞​(t,x)g(x)=V\_{T}(t,x)\\to V\_{\\infty}(t,x). Thus (t,x)∈𝒟(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{D} and so in total 𝒟=𝒟∞\\mathcal{D}=\\mathcal{D}^{\\infty} which yields τ∞​(t,x)=τ∞∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\\infty}(t,x)=\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x).\
\
Lastly, to show that τ∞∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x) is an optimal stopping time, we first show that τ∞∗​(t,x)∈𝒯\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{T}.\
For finite T>tT>t, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0τT∗​(t,x)c⁡(t+u)​𝑑u\]≤VT​(t,x)≤‖g‖∞.\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x)}c(t+u)\\,du\\right\]\\leq V\_{T}(t,x)\\leq\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty}. |  |\
\
Using monotone convergence, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∞∗​(t,x)c⁡(t+u)​𝑑u\]≤‖g‖∞,\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x)}c(t+u)\\,du\\right\]\\leq\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty}, |  |\
\
and so τ∞∗​(t,x)∈𝒯\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{T} by Proposition [3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4. ‣ 3.3. Basic consequences of the setup ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
To show that the stopping time achieves optimality, we utilize monotone convergence as above and dominated convergence on the bounded gg to get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V∞​(t,x)=limT→∞VT​(t,x)=𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∞∗​(t,x)c⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτ∞∗​(t,x)x)\].\\displaystyle V\_{\\infty}(t,x)=\\lim\_{T\\to\\infty}V\_{T}(t,x)=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x)}c(t+u)\\,du+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x)})\\right\]. |  |\
\
Thus τ∞∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t,x) is admissible and attains the infinite-horizon value as claimed.\
∎\
\
Next, we show a technical lemma for the spatial concavity of the value function in (t,x)(t,x) coordinates.\
\
###### Lemma 6.2.\
\
For every t∈\[0,T)t\\in\[0,T) the map x↦VT​(t,x)x\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is concave on \[0,1\]\[0,1\] for T≤∞T\\leq\\infty.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Consider first T<∞T<\\infty. If t=Tt=T, then VT​(t,x)=g​(x)V\_{T}(t,x)=g(x), hence concave. Fix a t∈\[0,T)t\\in\[0,T). We first give a probabilistic proof that for deterministic times the transition semigroup operator on XxX^{x} preserves concavity. Let (Ps)s≥0(P\_{s})\_{s\\geq 0} denote the transition semigroup of the Markov process XxX^{x}, defined by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Ps​f​(x)=𝔼⁡\[f⁡(Xsx)\]P\_{s}f(x)=\\mathbb{E}\[f(X^{x}\_{s})\] |  |\
\
for appropriate test functions ff.\
\
Let Zsx:=∂xXsxZ^{x}\_{s}:=\\partial\_{x}X^{x}\_{s}. By Assumption [B.(v)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i5 "In Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), (Zsx)s∈\[0,T\](Z^{x}\_{s})\_{s\\in\[0,T\]} is a true exponential martingale and 𝔼⁡\[Zsx\]=1\\mathbb{E}\[Z^{x}\_{s}\]=1 for all x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1) and s∈\[0,T\]s\\in\[0,T\].\
Assume f∈Cb1​(\[0,1\])f\\in C^{1}\_{b}(\[0,1\]).\
Let x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1) and take h∈\[0,1−x)h\\in\[0,1-x). The integral remainder formula yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | f⁡(Xtx+h)−f⁡(Xtx)=h​∫01f′​(Xtx+r​h)​Ztx+r​h​𝑑rf(X^{x+h}\_{t})-f(X^{x}\_{t})=h\\int\_{0}^{1}f^{\\prime}(X^{x+rh}\_{t})Z^{x+rh}\_{t}dr |  |\
\
and so the difference quotient of the semigroup operator is given by\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.2) |  | Pt​f​(x+h)−Pt​f​(x)h=∫01𝔼⁡\[f′​(Xtx+r​h)​Ztx+r​h\]​𝑑r,\\frac{P\_{t}f(x+h)-P\_{t}f(x)}{h}=\\int\_{0}^{1}\\mathbb{E}\[f^{\\prime}(X^{x+rh}\_{t})Z^{x+rh}\_{t}\]dr, |  |\
\
by Fubini since f′f^{\\prime} is bounded and ZtyZ^{y}\_{t} is positive and has expectation 1. Fix an r∈\[0,1\]r\\in\[0,1\]. Then\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.3) |  | 𝔼​\|f′​(Xtx+r​h)​Ztx+r​h−f′​(Xtx)​Ztx\|≤‖f′‖∞​𝔼​\|Ztx+r​h−Ztx\|+𝔼⁡\[\|f′​(Xtx+r​h)−f′​(Xtx)\|​Ztx\].\\mathbb{E}\|f^{\\prime}(X^{x+rh}\_{t})Z^{x+rh}\_{t}-f^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{t})Z^{x}\_{t}\|\\leq\\\|f^{\\prime}\\\|\_{\\infty}\\mathbb{E}\|Z^{x+rh}\_{t}-Z^{x}\_{t}\|+\\mathbb{E}\[\|f^{\\prime}(X^{x+rh}\_{t})-f^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{t})\|Z^{x}\_{t}\]. |  |\
\
The first term goes to 00 by Scheffé’s lemma since y↦Ztyy\\mapsto Z^{y}\_{t} is almost surely continuous, ZyZ^{y} is positive, and 𝔼⁡\[Ztx+r​h\]=𝔼⁡\[Ztx\]=1\\mathbb{E}\[Z^{x+rh}\_{t}\]=\\mathbb{E}\[Z^{x}\_{t}\]=1. The second term goes to 00 by dominated convergence since f′f^{\\prime} is bounded and y↦f′​(Xty)y\\mapsto f^{\\prime}(X^{y}\_{t}) is almost surely continuous. Hence\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 𝔼⁡\[f′​(Xtx+r​h)​Ztx+r​h\]→𝔼⁡\[f′​(Xtx)​Ztx\],\\mathbb{E}\[f^{\\prime}(X^{x+rh}\_{t})Z^{x+rh}\_{t}\]\\to\\mathbb{E}\[f^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{t})Z^{x}\_{t}\], |  |\
\
for any r∈\[0,1\]r\\in\[0,1\]. Then, by another application of dominated convergence, and a symmetric argument for the left difference quotient, we get\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.4) |  | ∂x(Pt​f)​(x)=𝔼⁡\[f′​(Xtx)​Ztx\].\\partial\_{x}(P\_{t}f)(x)=\\mathbb{E}\[f^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{t})Z^{x}\_{t}\]. |  |\
\
As argued above, ZxZ^{x} is a likelihood process and thus Girsanov’s theorem induces a new measure ℚx\\mathbb{Q}^{x} on ℱt\\mathcal{F}\_{t} by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | d​ℚxd​ℙ\|ℱt=Ztx,\\frac{d\\mathbb{Q}^{x}}{d\\mathbb{P}}\\Big\|\_{\\mathcal{F}\_{t}}=Z^{x}\_{t}, |  |\
\
where\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Wsx=Bs−∫0sσ′​(Xux)​𝑑uW^{x}\_{s}=B\_{s}-\\int\_{0}^{s}\\sigma^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{u})\\,du |  |\
\
for s∈\[0,t\]s\\in\[0,t\] is a ℚx\\mathbb{Q}^{x}–Brownian motion. The process XxX^{x} satisfies X0x=xX^{x}\_{0}=x and\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | d​Xsx=σ′​(Xsx)​σ​(Xsx)​d​s+σ⁡(Xsx)​d​Wsx,dX^{x}\_{s}=\\sigma^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{s})\\sigma(X^{x}\_{s})\\,ds+\\sigma(X^{x}\_{s})\\,dW^{x}\_{s}, |  |\
\
and ( [6.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E4 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) becomes\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.5) |  | ∂xPt​f​(x)=𝔼ℚx​\[f′​(Xtx)\].\\partial\_{x}P\_{t}f(x)=\\mathbb{E}^{\\mathbb{Q}^{x}}\[f^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{t})\]. |  |\
\
Let Yx=(Ytx)t∈\[0,T\]Y^{x}=(Y^{x}\_{t})\_{t\\in\[0,T\]} be the unique strong solution, on some Wiener space (Ω,𝒢,𝔾,ℚ)(\\Omega,\\mathcal{G},\\mathbb{G},\\mathbb{Q}) carrying a Brownian motion W=(Wt)t∈\[0,T\]W=(W\_{t})\_{t\\in\[0,T\]}, of\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | d​Ytx=σ′​(Ytx)​σ​(Ytx)​d​t+σ⁡(Ytx)​d​Wt,dY^{x}\_{t}=\\sigma^{\\prime}(Y^{x}\_{t})\\sigma(Y^{x}\_{t})dt+\\sigma(Y^{x}\_{t})dW\_{t}, |  |\
\
with Y0x=xY^{x}\_{0}=x. Then, since the law of XxX^{x} under ℚx\\mathbb{Q}^{x} is equal to the law of YxY^{x} under ℚ\\mathbb{Q}, we can write ( [6.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E5 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) as\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ∂xPt​f​(x)=𝔼ℚ​\[f′​(Ytx)\].\\partial\_{x}P\_{t}f(x)=\\mathbb{E}^{\\mathbb{Q}}\[f^{\\prime}(Y^{x}\_{t})\]. |  |\
\
By the comparison principle, see e.g. \[ [21](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib21 "")\], we have that x↦Ytxx\\mapsto Y^{x}\_{t} is increasing almost surely. If ff is concave, then x↦f′​(Ytx)x\\mapsto f^{\\prime}(Y^{x}\_{t}) is almost surely decreasing, and hence x↦∂xPt​f​(x)x\\mapsto\\partial\_{x}P\_{t}f(x) is decreasing. Therefore, we conclude x↦Pt​f​(x)x\\mapsto P\_{t}f(x) is concave.\
\
Next, assume ff is concave with 0≤f⁡(x)≤g⁡(x)0\\leq f(x)\\leq g(x) for x∈\[0,1\]x\\in\[0,1\]. There exists a sequence (fn)n∈ℕ⊂Cb1​(\[0,1\])(f\_{n})\_{n\\in\\mathbb{N}}\\subset C^{1}\_{b}(\[0,1\]) of concave functions such that fn→ff\_{n}\\to f uniformly. By the triangle inequality, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ‖Pt​fn−Pt​f‖∞≤‖fn−f‖∞,\|\|P\_{t}f\_{n}-P\_{t}f\|\|\_{\\infty}\\leq\|\|f\_{n}-f\|\|\_{\\infty}, |  |\
\
so Pt​fn→Pt​fP\_{t}f\_{n}\\to P\_{t}f uniformly. Noting that uniform convergence respects concavity, we have x↦Pt​f​(x)x\\mapsto P\_{t}f(x) is concave on (0,1)(0,1). Since ff is bounded between 00 and gg, and g⁡(x)=0g(x)=0 if and only if x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\}, it holds that x↦Pt​f​(x)x\\mapsto P\_{t}f(x) is concave on \[0,1\]\[0,1\].\
\
Next, we will upgrade this to hold for stopping times. Specifically, we first prove this for discrete stopping times in the Bermudan approximation. Define for n∈ℕn\\in\\mathbb{N} the dyadic Bermudan version of ( [3.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E15 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), namely, VTn​(t,x):=vTn​(0,x)V\_{T}^{n}(t,x):=v^{n}\_{T}(0,x) where\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | vTn​(k,x):=infτ∈𝒯T−tn,k𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+k​Δ+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Xτx)\]v^{n}\_{T}(k,x):=\\inf\_{\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}^{n,k}\_{T-t}}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+k\\Delta+u)\\,du+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\\right\] |  |\
\
for k=0,…,2nk=0,\\ldots,2^{n}, where Δ=(T−t)​2−n\\Delta=(T-t)2^{-n} and 𝒯T−tn,k\\mathcal{T}^{n,k}\_{T-t} is the set of stopping times taking values in {0,Δ,…,(2n−k)​Δ}\\{0,\\Delta,\\dots,(2^{n}-k)\\Delta\\}.\
We will denote 𝒯T−tn:=𝒯T−tn,0\\mathcal{T}^{n}\_{T-t}:=\\mathcal{T}^{n,0}\_{T-t} for simplicity.\
By the strong Markov property and the fact that the running cost is deterministic, the dynamic programming principle implies\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | vTn​(k,x)=min⁡{g⁡(x),∫0Δc⁡(t+k​Δ+u)​𝑑u+PΔ​vTn​(k+1,⋅)​(x)}v^{n}\_{T}(k,x)=\\min\\left\\{g(x),\\int\_{0}^{\\Delta}c(t+k\\Delta+u)\\,du+P\_{\\Delta}v^{n}\_{T}(k+1,\\cdot)(x)\\right\\} |  |\
\
for k=0,…,2n−1k=0,\\dots,2^{n}-1 with vTn​(2n,x)=g⁡(x)v^{n}\_{T}(2^{n},x)=g(x). We proceed to prove concavity of x↦vTn​(k,x)x\\mapsto v^{n}\_{T}(k,x) by backward induction. At k=2nk=2^{n} concavity holds by concavity of gg. For k<2nk<2^{n}, assume x↦vTn​(k+1,x)x\\mapsto v^{n}\_{T}(k+1,x) is concave. Then PΔ​vTn​(k+1,⋅)​(x)P\_{\\Delta}v^{n}\_{T}(k+1,\\cdot)(x) is concave and adding the running cost preserves the concavity. The pointwise minimum of two concave functions is concave. Hence x↦vTn​(k,x)x\\mapsto v^{n}\_{T}(k,x) is concave. By backward induction, it then holds that x↦vTn​(k,x)x\\mapsto v^{n}\_{T}(k,x) is concave for any k∈{0,1,…,2n}k\\in\\{0,1,\\dots,2^{n}\\}, and so x↦VTn​(t,x)x\\mapsto V^{n}\_{T}(t,x) is concave.\
\
Next, we pass from the Bermudan approximation to the continuous-time problem. Since 𝒯T−tn⊆𝒯T−tn+1⊆𝒯T−t\\mathcal{T}^{n}\_{T-t}\\subseteq\\mathcal{T}^{n+1}\_{T-t}\\subseteq\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t}, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | VTn​(t,x)≥VTn+1​(t,x)≥VT​(t,x)V^{n}\_{T}(t,x)\\geq V^{n+1}\_{T}(t,x)\\geq V\_{T}(t,x) |  |\
\
for x∈\[0,1\]x\\in\[0,1\]. Hence, for each xx, the limit\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V¯T​(t,x):=limn→∞VTn​(t,x)\\overline{V}\_{T}(t,x):=\\lim\_{n\\to\\infty}V^{n}\_{T}(t,x) |  |\
\
exists and satisfies V¯T​(t,x)≥VT​(t,x)\\overline{V}\_{T}(t,x)\\geq V\_{T}(t,x). To prove the converse, fix τ∈𝒯T−t\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t} and define the dyadic approximation τn:=inf{k​Δn:k​Δn≥τ}∧(T−t)\\tau\_{n}:=\\inf\\{k\\Delta\_{n}:k\\Delta\_{n}\\geq\\tau\\}\\wedge(T-t) where Δn:=(T−t)​2−n\\Delta\_{n}:=(T-t)2^{-n}. Then τn∈𝒯T−tn\\tau\_{n}\\in\\mathcal{T}^{n}\_{T-t} gives τn↘τ\\tau\_{n}\\searrow\\tau almost surely. Since XxX^{x} has continuous paths and the integrated cost, u↦Ft​(u):=∫0uc⁡(t+r)​𝑑ru\\mapsto F\_{t}(u):=\\int\_{0}^{u}c(t+r)dr, is continuous, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Ft​(τn)+g⁡(Xτnx)→Ft​(τ)+g⁡(Xτx),F\_{t}(\\tau\_{n})+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau\_{n}})\\to F\_{t}(\\tau)+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau}), |  |\
\
and dominated convergence yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V¯T​(t,x)=limn→∞VTn​(t,x)≤limn→∞𝔼⁡\[Ft​(τn)+g⁡(Xτnx)\]=𝔼⁡\[Ft​(τ)+g⁡(Xτx)\].\\overline{V}\_{T}(t,x)=\\lim\_{n\\to\\infty}V^{n}\_{T}(t,x)\\leq\\lim\_{n\\to\\infty}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[F\_{t}(\\tau\_{n})+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau\_{n}})\\right\]=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[F\_{t}(\\tau)+g(X^{x}\_{\\tau})\\right\]. |  |\
\
Since τ∈𝒯T−t\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t} was arbitrary, taking the infimum over τ\\tau yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | V¯T​(t,x)≤VT​(t,x).\\overline{V}\_{T}(t,x)\\leq V\_{T}(t,x). |  |\
\
Thus VTn​(t,x)↘VT​(t,x)V^{n}\_{T}(t,x)\\searrow V\_{T}(t,x) pointwise as n→∞n\\to\\infty. Pointwise limits of concave functions preserve concavity, and hence x↦VT​(t,x)x\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is concave.\
\
For T=∞T=\\infty, we use the pointwise convergence from Proposition [6.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 6.1. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and conclude x↦V∞​(t,x)x\\mapsto V\_{\\infty}(t,x) is concave.\
∎\
\
From concavity, we get local Lipschitz regularity. However, we will need explicit Lipschitz bounds and a probabilistic representation of the derivative in finite horizon, when it exists. When g′g^{\\prime} is bounded, under Assumption [B.(iii).(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i1 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), the argument in the proof of Lemma [6.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Lemma 6.2. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") yielding ( [6.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E4 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) gives the representation by exchanging tt with a bounded stopping time. In the case of unbounded g′g^{\\prime} the argument is less clear. To this end, we prove the following lemma.\
\
###### Lemma 6.3.\
\
Let \[A,B\]⊂(0,1)\[A,B\]\\subset(0,1) and T1<∞T\_{1}<\\infty be given. For any ε>0\\varepsilon>0 such that \[A−ε,B+ε\]⊂(0,1)\[A-\\varepsilon,B+\\varepsilon\]\\subset(0,1), there exists a δ>0\\delta>0 such that for all x,y∈\[A,B\]x,y\\in\[A,B\] with \|x−y\|<δ\|x-y\|<\\delta it holds that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|Xtx−Xty\|≤ε\|X^{x}\_{t}-X^{y}\_{t}\|\\leq\\varepsilon |  |\
\
for t≤inf{s≥0:Xsx∉(A,B)}∧T1t\\leq\\inf\\{s\\geq 0:X^{x}\_{s}\\not\\in(A,B)\\}\\wedge T\_{1}.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
We write\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | x~:=Ψ⁡(x),y~:=Ψ⁡(y),A~:=Ψ⁡(A),B~:=Ψ⁡(B).\\tilde{x}:=\\Psi(x),\\qquad\\tilde{y}:=\\Psi(y),\\qquad\\tilde{A}:=\\Psi(A),\\qquad\\tilde{B}:=\\Psi(B). |  |\
\
Define\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Dt:=Ltx~−Lty~=x~−y~+∫0t(μ⁡(Lsx~)−μ⁡(Lsy~))​𝑑s.D\_{t}:=L^{\\tilde{x}}\_{t}-L^{\\tilde{y}}\_{t}={\\tilde{x}}-{\\tilde{y}}+\\int\_{0}^{t}(\\mu(L^{\\tilde{x}}\_{s})-\\mu(L^{\\tilde{y}}\_{s}))\\,ds. |  |\
\
Let ε~>0\\tilde{\\varepsilon}>0 be such that \[A~−ε~,B~+ε~\]⊂Ψ⁡((0,1))\[\\tilde{A}-\\tilde{\\varepsilon},\\tilde{B}+\\tilde{\\varepsilon}\]\\subset\\Psi((0,1)) and consider ρ~=inf{s≥0:Lsx~∉\[A~,B~\]​ or ​Lsy~∉\[A~−ε~,B~+ε~\]}∧T1\\tilde{\\rho}=\\inf\\{s\\geq 0:L^{\\tilde{x}}\_{s}\\not\\in\[\\tilde{A},\\tilde{B}\]\\text{ or }L^{\\tilde{y}}\_{s}\\not\\in\[\\tilde{A}-\\tilde{\\varepsilon},\\tilde{B}+\\tilde{\\varepsilon}\]\\}\\wedge T\_{1}. Then, for t≤ρ~t\\leq\\tilde{\\rho}, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|Dt\|≤\|x~−y~\|+∫0t\|μ⁡(Lsx~)−μ⁡(Lsy~)\|​𝑑s≤\|x~−y~\|+Kε~​∫0t\|Ds\|​𝑑s,\|D\_{t}\|\\leq\|\\tilde{x}-{\\tilde{y}}\|+\\int\_{0}^{t}\|\\mu(L^{\\tilde{x}}\_{s})-\\mu(L^{\\tilde{y}}\_{s})\|\\,ds\\leq\|{\\tilde{x}}-{\\tilde{y}}\|+K\_{\\tilde{\\varepsilon}}\\int\_{0}^{t}\|D\_{s}\|\\,ds, |  |\
\
where Kε~K\_{\\tilde{\\varepsilon}} is a uniform Lipschitz constant for μ\\mu on \[A~−ε~,B~+ε~\]\[\\tilde{A}-\\tilde{\\varepsilon},\\tilde{B}+\\tilde{\\varepsilon}\]. By Grönwall’s inequality, we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|Dt\|≤\|x~−y~\|​et​Kε~≤\|x~−y~\|​eT1​Kε~.\|D\_{t}\|\\leq\|{\\tilde{x}}-{\\tilde{y}}\|e^{tK\_{\\tilde{\\varepsilon}}}\\leq\|{\\tilde{x}}-{\\tilde{y}}\|e^{T\_{1}K\_{\\tilde{\\varepsilon}}}. |  |\
\
So in total, we get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|Xtx−Xty\|≤KΨ−1​\|Ltx~−Lty~\|≤KΨ−1​\|x~−y~\|​eT1​Kε~≤KΨ−1​KΨ​eT1​Kε~​\|x−y\|\|X^{x}\_{t}-X^{y}\_{t}\|\\leq K\_{\\Psi^{-1}}\|L\_{t}^{\\tilde{x}}-L\_{t}^{\\tilde{y}}\|\\leq K\_{\\Psi^{-1}}\|{\\tilde{x}}-{\\tilde{y}}\|e^{T\_{1}K\_{\\tilde{\\varepsilon}}}\\leq K\_{\\Psi^{-1}}K\_{\\Psi}e^{T\_{1}K\_{\\tilde{\\varepsilon}}}\|x-y\| |  |\
\
for t≤ρ~t\\leq\\tilde{\\rho}, where KΨK\_{\\Psi} and KΨ−1K\_{\\Psi^{-1}} are Lipschitz constants for Ψ\\Psi and Ψ−1\\Psi^{-1}. Taking\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | δ≤min⁡{ε~,εKΨ−1}​1KΨ​e−T1​Kε~\\delta\\leq\\min\\left\\{\\tilde{\\varepsilon},\\frac{\\varepsilon}{K\_{\\Psi^{-1}}}\\right\\}\\frac{1}{K\_{\\Psi}}e^{-T\_{1}K\_{\\tilde{\\varepsilon}}} |  |\
\
proves the claim.\
∎\
\
Importantly, for a given\
ε>0\\varepsilon>0, the constant δ>0\\delta>0 appearing in Lemma [6.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem3 "Lemma 6.3. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") can be chosen _independently_ of the sample path.\
\
###### Proposition 6.4.\
\
Let T<∞T<\\infty. For every t∈\[0,T\]t\\in\[0,T\] the map x↦VT​(t,x)x\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is Liploc​((,,,))\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}((0,1)). Specifically, for the optimal stopping time τ∗:=τT∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\*}:=\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x),\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.6) |  | ∂xVT​(t,x)=𝔼⁡\[g′​(Xτ∗x)​∂xXτ∗x\],\\partial\_{x}V\_{T}(t,x)=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[g^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{\\tau^{\*}})\\partial\_{x}X^{x}\_{\\tau^{\*}}\\right\], |  |\
\
for almost every x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1). In particular, for x,y∈\[a,b\]⊂(0,1)x,y\\in\[a,b\]\\subset(0,1),\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.7) |  | \|VT​(t,x)−VT​(t,y)\|≤Kspace​\|x−y\|,\|V\_{T}(t,x)-V\_{T}(t,y)\|\\leq K\_{\\text{space}}\|x-y\|, |  |\
\
where Kspace:=‖g′‖∞K\_{\\text{space}}:=\\\|g^{\\prime}\\\|\_{\\infty} under Assumption [B.(iii).(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i1 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), and there exists an interval \[A,B\]⊂(0,1)\[A,B\]\\subset(0,1) independent of tt and TT such that Kspace:=‖g′‖L∞​(\[a,b\]∪\[A,B\])<∞K\_{\\text{space}}:=\\\|g^{\\prime}\\\|\_{L^{\\infty}(\[a,b\]\\cup\[A,B\])}<\\infty if Assumption [B.(iii).(b)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i2 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") holds.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
By Lemma [6.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Lemma 6.2. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), we get that x↦VT​(t,x)x\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is Liploc​((,,,))\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}((0,1)) since it is concave. We would like to repeat the arguments above from the proof of ( [6.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E4 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
If Assumption [B.(iii).(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i1 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") holds, then the argument leading to\
( [6.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E4 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) extends to every bounded stopping time\
τ∈𝒯T−t\\tau\\in\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t}. Indeed, by joint continuity of the stochastic flow,\

###### Proposition 6.6.\
\
For any T≤∞T\\leq\\infty and x∈\[0,1\]x\\in\[0,1\], it holds that t↦VT​(t,x)t\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is Liploc​(\[0,T))\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}(\[0,T)). Specifically, at points of differentiability it holds that\

###### Proof.\
\
We first take T<∞T<\\infty. If x∈{0,1}x\\in\\{0,1\\}, then the statement holds since τT∗​(t,x)=0\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x)=0 almost surely, so fix any x∈(0,1)x\\in(0,1). We will show the result through the Lagrange-formulated problem WTW\_{T} defined in ( [3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E8 "In 3.4. Transformations and subproblems ‣ 3. We will first prove ( [6.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E13 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) by bounding the left and right derivatives from below and above, respectively.\
\
Let t∈(0,T)t\\in(0,T) and h>0h>0 such that t−h≥0t-h\\geq 0, and assume, for simplicity, that h≤1h\\leq 1. Denote the optimal stopping time by τ∗:=τT∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\*}:=\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x). Then τ∗\\tau^{\*} is admissible for the problem with initial data (t−h,x)(t-h,x). We have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | WT​(t,x)−WT​(t−h,x)h≥𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∗c⁡(t+u)−c⁡(t−h+u)h​𝑑u\]=𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∗∫01c′​(t+u−r​h)​𝑑r​𝑑u\],\\frac{W\_{T}(t,x)-W\_{T}(t-h,x)}{h}\\geq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}}\\frac{c(t+u)-c(t-h+u)}{h}\\,du\\right\]=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}}\\int\_{0}^{1}c^{\\prime}(t+u-rh)\\,dr\\,du\\right\], |  |\
\
by the fundamental theorem of calculus. By ( [3.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E7 "In 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|c′​(t+u−r​h)\|≤K′​c​(t+u),\|c^{\\prime}(t+u-rh)\|\\leq K^{\\prime}c(t+u), |  |\
\
and since τ∗∈𝒯T−t⊂𝒯\\tau^{\*}\\in\\mathcal{T}\_{T-t}\\subset\\mathcal{T}, we have 𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∗c⁡(t+u)​𝑑u\]<∞\\mathbb{E}\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}}c(t+u)\\,du\]<\\infty, and\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ∫01c′​(t+u−r​h)​𝑑r→c′​(t+u)\\int\_{0}^{1}c^{\\prime}(t+u-rh)\\,dr\\to c^{\\prime}(t+u) |  |\
\
as h↘0h\\searrow 0,\
we can apply dominated convergence theorem to get\

|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.16) |  | lim infh↘0WT​(t,x)−WT​(t−h,x)h≥𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∗c′​(t+u)​𝑑u\]=𝔼⁡\[c⁡(t+τ∗)\]−c⁡(t).\\liminf\_{h\\searrow 0}\\frac{W\_{T}(t,x)-W\_{T}(t-h,x)}{h}\\geq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}}c^{\\prime}(t+u)\\,du\\right\]=\\mathbb{E}\[c(t+\\tau^{\*})\]-c(t). |  |\
\
For the upper bound on the right derivative we let t∈(0,T)t\\in(0,T) and h∈(0,1\]h\\in(0,1\] such that t+h≤Tt+h\\leq T. We note that τ∗\\tau^{\*} may be inadmissible for the problem with initial data (t+h,x)(t+h,x). Thus let uh=T−(t+h)u\_{h}=T-(t+h) and consider the stopping time τ∗∧uh\\tau^{\*}\\wedge u\_{h}. First, by the strong Markov property, the dynamic programming principle for the Lagrange-formulated problem yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | WT(t,x)=𝔼\[∫0τ∗∧uh(c(t+u)+ℒg(Xux))du+𝟏{τ∗>uh}WT(T−h,Xuhx)\],W\_{T}(t,x)=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\\wedge u\_{h}}\\bigl(c(t+u)+\\mathcal{L}g(X^{x}\_{u})\\bigr)\\,du+\\mathbf{1}\_{\\{\\tau^{\*}>u\_{h}\\}}W\_{T}(T-h,X^{x}\_{u\_{h}})\\right\], |  |\
\
and hence\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | WT(t+h,x)−WT(t,x)≤𝔼\[∫0τ∗∧uh(c(t+h+u)−c(t+u))du−𝟏{τ∗>uh}WT(T−h,Xuhx)\].W\_{T}(t+h,x)-W\_{T}(t,x)\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\\wedge u\_{h}}\\big(c(t+h+u)-c(t+u)\\big)\\,du-\\mathbf{1}\_{\\{\\tau^{\*}>u\_{h}\\}}W\_{T}(T-h,X^{x}\_{u\_{h}})\\right\]. |  |\
\
For any y∈(0,1)y\\in(0,1), it holds that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 0≤−1h​WT​(T−h,y)≤1h​𝔼​\[∫0h‖ℒ​g‖∞​𝑑u\]=K′′0\\leq-\\frac{1}{h}W\_{T}(T-h,y)\\leq\\frac{1}{h}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{h}\\\|\\mathcal{L}g\\\|\_{\\infty}\\,du\\right\]=K^{\\prime\\prime} |  |\
\
since −c⁡(u)≤0-c(u)\\leq 0 for all u≥0u\\geq 0 and thus\

\
Since uh↗T−tu\_{h}\\nearrow T-t and\

by dominated convergence, we get\

| (6.18) |  | lim suph↘0WT​(t+h,x)−WT​(t,x)h≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∗c′​(t+u)​𝑑u\]+K′′​ℙ​(τ∗=T−t).\\limsup\_{h\\searrow 0}\\frac{W\_{T}(t+h,x)-W\_{T}(t,x)}{h}\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}}c^{\\prime}(t+u)\\,du\\right\]+K^{\\prime\\prime}\\mathbb{P}(\\tau^{\*}=T-t). |  |\
\
Thus, if (t,x)(t,x) is a point of differentiability, we have ( [6.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E13 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. To prove the local Lipschitz property we need to bound the right and left derivatives from above and below, respectively. For the left derivative let again t∈(0,T)t\\in(0,T) and h∈(0,1\]h\\in(0,1\] such that t−h≥0t-h\\geq 0. Hence, similar to ( [6.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E17 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | WT​(t,x)−WT​(t−h,x)h≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τh∗∧(T−t)∫01c′​(t+u−r​h)​𝑑r​𝑑u\]+K′′​ℙ​(τh∗>T−t).\\frac{W\_{T}(t,x)-W\_{T}(t-h,x)}{h}\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{h}\\wedge(T-t)}\\int\_{0}^{1}c^{\\prime}(t+u-rh)\\,dr\\,du\\right\]+K^{\\prime\\prime}\\mathbb{P}(\\tau^{\*}\_{h}>T-t). |  |\
\

\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
|  |  | \|𝔼⁡\[∫0τh∗∧(T−t)∫01c′​(t+u−r​h)​𝑑r​𝑑u\]\|≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τh∗∧(T−t)∫01\|c′​((t−h+u)+(1−r)​h)\|​𝑑r​𝑑u\]\\displaystyle\\left\|\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{h}\\wedge(T-t)}\\int\_{0}^{1}c^{\\prime}(t+u-rh)\\,dr\\,du\\right\]\\right\|\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{h}\\wedge(T-t)}\\int\_{0}^{1}\\left\|c^{\\prime}((t-h+u)+(1-r)h)\\right\|\\,dr\\,du\\right\] |  |\
|  | ≤\\displaystyle\\leq | K′​𝔼​\[∫0τh∗∧(T−t)∫01c⁡(t−h+u)​𝑑r​𝑑u\]≤K′​𝔼​\[∫0τh∗c⁡(t−h+u)​𝑑u\]≤K′​‖g‖∞\\displaystyle K^{\\prime}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{h}\\wedge(T-t)}\\int\_{0}^{1}c(t-h+u)\\,dr\\,du\\right\]\\leq K^{\\prime}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{h}}c(t-h+u)\\,du\\right\]\\leq K^{\\prime}\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty} |  |\
\
since (1−r)​h≤1(1-r)h\\leq 1 and\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 0≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τT∗​(s,y)c⁡(s+u)​𝑑u\]≤sup(s,y)∈𝒮TVT​(s,y)≤‖g‖∞0\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(s,y)}c(s+u)\\,du\\right\]\\leq\\sup\_{(s,y)\\in\\mathcal{S}\_{T}}V\_{T}(s,y)\\leq\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty} |  |\
\
for any s∈\[0,T)s\\in\[0,T).\
Thus, since ℙ⁡(τh∗>T−t)≤1\\mathbb{P}(\\tau^{\*}\_{h}>T-t)\\leq 1, and recalling Ktime=K′​‖g‖∞+K′′K\_{\\text{time}}=K^{\\prime}\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty}+K^{\\prime\\prime}, we have\

|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.19) |  | \|𝔼⁡\[∫0τh∗∧(T−t)∫01c′​(t+u−r​h)​𝑑r​𝑑u\]+K′′​ℙ​(τh∗>T−t)\|≤Ktime.\\left\|\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{h}\\wedge(T-t)}\\int\_{0}^{1}c^{\\prime}(t+u-rh)\\,dr\\,du\\right\]+K^{\\prime\\prime}\\mathbb{P}(\\tau^{\*}\_{h}>T-t)\\right\|\\leq K\_{\\text{time}}. |  |\
\
Similarly, for the right derivative, letting τh∗:=τT∗​(t+h,x)\\tau^{\*}\_{h}:=\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t+h,x), we have\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.20) |  | WT​(t+h,x)−WT​(t,x)h≥𝔼⁡\[∫0τh∗∫01c′​(t+u+r​h)​𝑑r​𝑑u\]≥−K′​‖g‖∞,\\frac{W\_{T}(t+h,x)-W\_{T}(t,x)}{h}\\geq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{h}}\\int\_{0}^{1}c^{\\prime}(t+u+rh)\\,dr\\,du\\right\]\\geq-K^{\\prime}\\\|g\\\|\_{\\infty}, |  |\
\

Combining ( [6.16](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E16 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")),( [6.18](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E18 "In Proof. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|WT​(t,x)−WT​(s,x)\|≤Ktime​\|s−t\|,\|W\_{T}(t,x)-W\_{T}(s,x)\|\\leq K\_{\\text{time}}\|s-t\|, |  |\
\
for \|s−t\|≤1\|s-t\|\\leq 1, which again is equivalent to ( [6.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E15 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. \

Hence for the infinite-horizon problem, we get ( [6.14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E14 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) almost everywhere and ( [6.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E15 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")). Note that this derivative is well-defined since if τ∞∗​(t,x)=∞\\tau\_{\\infty}^{\*}(t,x)=\\infty with positive probability, then c⁡(s)→0c(s)\\to 0 as s→∞s\\to\\infty because τ∞∗​(t,x)∈𝒯\\tau\_{\\infty}^{\*}(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{T}.\
∎\
\
Combining Propositions [6.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 6.4. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and [6.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), we get that (t,x)↦VT​(t,x)(t,x)\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is locally Lipschitz.\
\
###### Theorem 6.7.\
\
The function (t,x)↦VT​(t,x)(t,x)\\mapsto V\_{T}(t,x) is Liploc​(𝒮T)\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}(\\mathcal{S}\_{T}) for T≤∞T\\leq\\infty.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
First, let T<∞T<\\infty and consider (tn,xn)→(t,x)∈\[0,T)×(0,1)(t\_{n},x\_{n})\\to(t,x)\\in\[0,T)\\times(0,1) such that \|tn−t\|<1\\lvert t\_{n}-t\\rvert<1 and xn∈\[a,b\]⊂(0,1)x\_{n}\\in\[a,b\]\\subset(0,1) for all n∈ℕn\\in\\mathbb{N}. By ( [6.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E15 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) and ( [6.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E7 "In Proposition 6.4. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), we have\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|VT​(tn,xn)−VT​(t,x)\|\\displaystyle\\lvert V\_{T}(t\_{n},x\_{n})-V\_{T}(t,x)\\rvert | ≤\|VT​(tn,xn)−VT​(t,xn)\|+\|VT​(t,xn)−VT​(t,x)\|\\displaystyle\\leq\\lvert V\_{T}(t\_{n},x\_{n})-V\_{T}(t,x\_{n})\\rvert+\\lvert V\_{T}(t,x\_{n})-V\_{T}(t,x)\\rvert |  |\
|  |  | ≤Ktime\|tn−t​\|+Kspace\|​xn−x\|≤2​(Ktime+Kspace)​‖(tn,xn)−(t,x)‖2,\\displaystyle\\leq K\_{\\text{time}}\|t\_{n}-t\|+K\_{\\text{space}}\|x\_{n}-x\|\\leq 2(K\_{\\text{time}}+K\_{\\text{space}})\\\|(t\_{n},x\_{n})-(t,x)\\\|\_{2}, |  |\
\
which, we note, is uniform in TT. Thus, for the infinite-horizon problem we have\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | \|V∞​(t,x)−V∞​(s,y)\|=limT→∞\|VT​(t,x)−VT​(s,y)\|≤2​(Ktime+Kspace)​‖(t,x)−(s,y)‖2,\|V\_{\\infty}(t,x)-V\_{\\infty}(s,y)\|=\\lim\_{T\\to\\infty}\|V\_{T}(t,x)-V\_{T}(s,y)\|\\leq 2(K\_{\\text{time}}+K\_{\\text{space}})\\\|(t,x)-(s,y)\\\|\_{2}, |  |\
\
by Proposition [6.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 6.1. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and so we are done.\
∎\
\
With the Lipschitz regularity of the value function obtained in this section we are able to strengthen the pointwise convergence result of Proposition [6.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 6.1. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
\
###### Corollary 6.8.\
\
For every ℐ=\[t1,t2\]⊂\[0,∞)\\mathcal{I}=\[t\_{1},t\_{2}\]\\subset\[0,\\infty) and 𝒥=\[a¯,a¯\]⊂(0,1)\\mathcal{J}=\[\\underline{a},\\overline{a}\]\\subset(0,1) it holds that\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | VT→V∞V\_{T}\\to V\_{\\infty} |  |\
\
uniformly on ℐ×𝒥\\mathcal{I}\\times\\mathcal{J} for T→∞T\\to\\infty.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Proposition [6.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 6.1. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") yields pointwise monotone convergence, so Dini’s theorem yields the result since (t,x)↦V∞​(t,x)(t,x)\\mapsto V\_{\\infty}(t,x) is continuous by Theorem [6.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem7 "Theorem 6.7. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
∎\
\
The conclusions of Proposition [6.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 6.4. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and [6.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), and Theorem [6.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem7 "Theorem 6.7. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") can be directly translated into the Lamperti-transformed problem since Ψ−1\\Psi^{-1} is locally regular. We present the result needed for the Lagrange formulation of the Lamperti-transformed problem on finite horizon.\
\
###### Corollary 6.9.\
\
Let T<∞T<\\infty. The function (t,l)↦W~T​(t,l)(t,l)\\mapsto\\tilde{W}\_{T}(t,l) is Liploc​(𝒮~T)\\mathrm{Lip}\_{\\mathrm{loc}}(\\tilde{\\mathcal{S}}\_{T}). Specifically, at points of differentiability it holds that\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.21) |  | ∂lW~T​(t,l)=𝔼⁡\[∫0τ~∗∂lLul​∂l(ℒ~​g~)​(Lul)​𝑑u\]\\partial\_{l}\\tilde{W}\_{T}(t,l)=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tilde{\\tau}^{\*}}\\partial\_{l}L^{l}\_{u}\\partial\_{l}(\\tilde{\\mathcal{L}}\\tilde{g})(L^{l}\_{u})\\,du\\right\] |  |\
\
where τ~∗:=τ~T∗​(t,l)\\tilde{\\tau}^{\*}:=\\tilde{\\tau}^{\*}\_{T}(t,l) is the optimal stopping time, and\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.22) |  | 𝔼⁡\[c⁡(t+τ~∗)\]−c⁡(t)≤∂tW~T​(t,l)≤𝔼⁡\[c⁡(t+τ~∗)\]−c⁡(t)+K′′​ℙ​(τ~∗=T−t),\\mathbb{E}\[c(t+\\tilde{\\tau}^{\*})\]-c(t)\\leq\\partial\_{t}\\tilde{W}\_{T}(t,l)\\leq\\mathbb{E}\[c(t+\\tilde{\\tau}^{\*})\]-c(t)+K^{\\prime\\prime}\\mathbb{P}(\\tilde{\\tau}^{\*}=T-t), |  |\
\
where K′′K^{\\prime\\prime} is defined in Proposition [6.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
\
###### Proof.\
\
The first two claims hold by applying the chain rule to Theorem [6.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem7 "Theorem 6.7. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and Corollary [6.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem5 "Corollary 6.5. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). The latter holds by Proposition [6.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
∎\
\
The bounds on the time derivative in ( [6.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E13 "In Proposition 6.6. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) and ( [6.22](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E22 "In Corollary 6.9. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) are useful for the analysis below. However, we can in fact get an exact stochastic representation for the time derivative in the finite-horizon case as well, by a technique similar to \[ [22](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib22 "")\].\
\
###### Theorem 6.10.\
\
Let T<∞T<\\infty. It holds that\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.23) |  | ∂tVT​(t,x)=\\displaystyle\\partial\_{t}V\_{T}(t,x)= | 𝔼⁡\[c⁡(t+τ∗)\]−c⁡(t)\\displaystyle\\mathbb{E}\[c(t+\\tau^{\*})\]-c(t) |  |\
|  |  | −1T−t​𝔼​\[c⁡(t+τ∗)​τ∗−12​g′​(Xτ∗x)​∂xXτ∗x​{∫0τ∗σ′​(Xsx)​σ​(Xsx)∂xXsx​𝑑s−∫0τ∗σ⁡(Xsx)∂xXsx​d​Bs}\],\\displaystyle-\\frac{1}{T-t}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[c(t+\\tau^{\*})\\tau^{\*}-\\frac{1}{2}g^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{\\tau^{\*}})\\partial\_{x}X^{x}\_{\\tau^{\*}}\\left\\{\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}}\\tfrac{\\sigma^{\\prime}(X^{x}\_{s})\\sigma(X^{x}\_{s})}{\\partial\_{x}X^{x}\_{s}}\\,ds-\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}}\\tfrac{\\sigma(X^{x}\_{s})}{\\partial\_{x}X^{x}\_{s}}\\,dB\_{s}\\right\\}\\right\], |  |\
\
for almost every (t,x)∈𝒮T(t,x)\\in\\mathcal{S}\_{T} where τ∗:=τT∗​(t,x)\\tau^{\*}:=\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t,x).\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Take any (t,l)∈𝒮~T(t,l)\\in\\tilde{\\mathcal{S}}\_{T}. Let 𝒯\[0,1\](T−t)\\mathcal{T}^{(T-t)}\_{\[0,1\]} denote the set of stopping times with respect to the filtration (ℱs⁡(T−t))s∈\[0,1\](\\mathcal{F}\_{s(T-t)})\_{s\\in\[0,1\]}. Then\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (6.24) |  | V~T​(t,l)\\displaystyle\\tilde{V}\_{T}(t,l) | =infτ≤T−t𝔼⁡\[∫0τc⁡(t+u)​𝑑u+g~​(Lτl)\]\\displaystyle=\\inf\_{\\tau\\leq T-t}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau}c(t+u)\\,du+\\tilde{g}(L\_{\\tau}^{l})\\right\] |  |\
