Since b⁡(t+u)>γ⁡(t+u)b(t+u)>\\gamma(t+u), we have H⁡(t+u,Xux)>0H(t+u,X\_{u}^{x})>0 on this event. Thus, the integral in ( [7.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S7.E7 "In Proof of Theorem . ‣ 7. Free-Boundary Integral Equation ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) above is nonnegative. Furthermore, the local nondegeneracy gives positive probability that the process enters RR and spends positive Lebesgue time there. The first expectation is therefore strictly positive, which is a contradiction. Hence β⁡(t)≤b⁡(t)\\beta(t)\\leq b(t) for all t<Tt<T.\
\
Now assume for contradiction that there exists a t<Tt<T such that β⁡(t)<b⁡(t)\\beta(t)<b(t). Taking x=b⁡(t)x=b(t), we thus get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 0=WT(t,x)−WTβ(t,x)=𝔼\[∫0T−tH(t+u,Xux)𝟏{Xxu∈𝒞bt+u∖𝒞βt+u}du\].0=W\_{T}(t,x)-W\_{T}^{\\beta}(t,x)=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{T-t}H(t+u,X^{x}\_{u})\\mathbf{1}\_{\\{X^{x}\_{u}\\in\\mathcal{C}^{b}\_{t+u}\\setminus\\mathcal{C}^{\\beta}\_{t+u}\\}}\\,du\\right\]. |  |\
\
Again, H>0H>0 on 𝒞ub∖𝒞uβ\\mathcal{C}^{b}\_{u}\\setminus\\mathcal{C}^{\\beta}\_{u} for any u∈(t,T)u\\in(t,T) and there is nonzero probability of staying in that set for nonzero Lebesgue time. This gives another contradiction, and we conclude that β⁡(t)=b⁡(t)\\beta(t)=b(t).\
∎\
\
## 8\. Examples\
\
We now illustrate the preceding results for the win-martingales introduced in Section [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2 "2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). The main\
examples are the Aldous, Bass, and sequential testing win-martingales, whose time-changed volatility coefficients are\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | σA​(x)=sin⁡(π​x)π,σB​(x)=φ⁡(Φ−1​(x)),σI​(x)=x⁡(1−x).\\sigma\_{A}(x)=\\frac{\\sin(\\pi x)}{\\pi},\\qquad\\sigma\_{B}(x)=\\varphi(\\Phi^{-1}(x)),\\qquad\\sigma\_{I}(x)=x(1-x). |  |\
\
We consider the terminal costs induced by optimal actions as in Section [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2 "2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"):\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | gL2​(x)=x⁡(1−x),gCE​(x)=−x​log⁡x−(1−x)​log⁡(1−x),g\_{L^{2}}(x)=x(1-x),\\qquad g\_{\\mathrm{CE}}(x)=-x\\log x-(1-x)\\log(1-x), |  |\
\
where we note that gCEg\_{\\mathrm{CE}} fails to obey Assumption [B.(iii).(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i1 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), and thus, we have to make use of Assumption [B.(iii).(b)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i2 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") for cross-entropy loss.\
\
### 8.1. Constant optimal stopping boundaries\
\
###### Example 8.1.\
\
We first compare the Aldous and Bass win-martingales in the homogeneous infinite-horizon setting.\
The function ρ\\rho is given by ρ⁡(s)=1/S−s\\rho(s)=1/\\sqrt{S-s}\
so the time transformation in ( [2.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E5 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) is\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | A⁡(s)=∫0s1S−u​𝑑u=−log⁡(S−sS),Γ⁡(t)=S⁡(1−e−t),A(s)=\\int\_{0}^{s}\\frac{1}{S-u}\\,du=-\\log\\left(\\frac{S-s}{S}\\right),\\qquad\\Gamma(t)=S(1-e^{-t}), |  |\
\
and hence T=∞T=\\infty. If\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | f⁡(s)=c0S−s,f(s)=\\frac{c\_{0}}{S-s}, |  |\
\
then c⁡(t)≡c0c(t)\\equiv c\_{0} by ( [2.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E7 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
Thus, the time-changed problem is precisely the homogeneous infinite-horizon problem studied in\
Section [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5 "5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). The optimal rule is therefore the first exit time from a fixed interval given by Theorem [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 5.2. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
\
In Figure [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 8.1. Constant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") we take c0=0.075c\_{0}=0.075 and use the L2L^{2}–loss.\
The dotted curve corresponds to the Aldous win-martingale and the dashed curve\
to the Bass win-martingale. Numerically, the Aldous value function lies above the Bass value function, meaning\
that, under the same terminal loss and running cost, the Aldous game is more costly to predict\
optimally. In this sense it is the more difficult, or more exciting, game to call.\
\
Figure 1. Numerical plot of the value function x↦V⁡(x)x\\mapsto V(x) for the Aldous (dotted) and Bass (dashed) win-martingales with\
L2L^{2}–loss and constant transformed running cost c0=0.075c\_{0}=0.075. The vertical lines indicate the\
optimal stopping boundaries from Theorem [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 5.2. ‣ 5. Homogeneous Infinite-Horizon Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), and the solid curve is x↦gL2​(x)x\\mapsto g\_{L^{2}}(x).\
\
### 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries\
\
If the transformed running cost is monotone, then the optimal stopping boundary inherits a\
corresponding monotonicity. This monotonicity is determined by the cost-to-noise ratio\
f/ρ2f/\\rho^{2}.\
\
###### Proposition 8.2.\
\
Assume Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") for the time transformed problem. If s↦f⁡(s)/ρ2​(s)s\\mapsto f(s)/\\rho^{2}(s) is increasing, then the continuation region of the original problem is shrinking. Equivalently, t↦bT​(t)t\\mapsto b\_{T}(t) is decreasing.\
\
If A⁡(s)→∞A(s)\\to\\infty as s→Ss\\to S and s↦f⁡(s)/ρ2​(s)s\\mapsto f(s)/\\rho^{2}(s) is decreasing, then the continuation region of the original problem is expanding. Equivalently, t↦b⁡(t)t\\mapsto b(t) is increasing.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
By the definition of the time change and ( [2.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E7 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")),\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | c⁡(t)=f⁡(Γ⁡(t))​Γ′​(t)=f⁡(Γ⁡(t))ρ2​(Γ​(t)).c(t)=f(\\Gamma(t))\\Gamma^{\\prime}(t)=\\frac{f(\\Gamma(t))}{\\rho^{2}(\\Gamma(t))}. |  |\
\
Since Γ\\Gamma is increasing, cc has the same monotonicity as f/ρ2f/\\rho^{2}.\
\
Suppose first that cc is increasing and let t1≤t2<Tt\_{1}\\leq t\_{2}<T. Using an optimal stopping time for\
the problem started from (t2,x)(t\_{2},x) as an admissible stopping time for the problem started from\
(t1,x)(t\_{1},x), we obtain WT​(t2,x)≥WT​(t1,x)W\_{T}(t\_{2},x)\\geq W\_{T}(t\_{1},x) by arguing as in ( [6.35](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.E35 "In Proof. ‣ 6.2. Properties of the optimal stopping boundary ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
Thus, if (t1,x)∈𝒟T(t\_{1},x)\\in\\mathcal{D}\_{T}, then 0≥WT​(t2,x)≥WT​(t1,x)=00\\geq W\_{T}(t\_{2},x)\\geq W\_{T}(t\_{1},x)=0, so it follows\
that (t2,x)∈𝒟T(t\_{2},x)\\in\\mathcal{D}\_{T}. Therefore, the stopping set expands with time, and t↦bT​(t)t\\mapsto b\_{T}(t) is\
decreasing.\
\
If T=∞T=\\infty and cc is decreasing, the reverse comparison gives\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | W∞​(t2,x)−W∞​(t1,x)≤𝔼⁡\[∫0τ∞∗​(t1,x)(c⁡(t2+u)−c⁡(t1+u))​𝑑u\]≤0.W\_{\\infty}(t\_{2},x)-W\_{\\infty}(t\_{1},x)\\leq\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau^{\*}\_{\\infty}(t\_{1},x)}\\big(c(t\_{2}+u)-c(t\_{1}+u)\\big)\\,du\\right\]\\leq 0. |  |\
\
Hence the continuation set expands, and t↦b⁡(t)t\\mapsto b(t) is increasing.\
∎\
\
###### Remark 8.3.\
\
It should be noted that for the second case of Proposition [8.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 8.2. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") it\
is necessary that we assume T=∞T=\\infty. If T<∞T<\\infty, then τT∗​(t1,x)\\tau^{\*}\_{T}(t\_{1},x) may not be admissible for the problem with initial data (t2,x)(t\_{2},x).\
In fact, the set of stopping times over which the infimum is taken decreases, and so one cannot expect to get a lower value alone from the decrease in the temporal cost.\
\
Thus, the monotonicity of the continuation region is governed by f/ρ2f/\\rho^{2}, not by ff alone.\
In particular, an increasing running cost in the original time scale may still lead to an expanding\
continuation region if the noise level increases sufficiently fast.\
\
###### Example 8.4.\
\
We illustrate this point for the classical sequential testing win-martingale on the horizon S=1S=1 with the same time scaling\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | d​ΠsI=ΠsI​(1−ΠsI)S−s​d​Ws,d\\Pi\_{s}^{I}=\\frac{\\Pi\_{s}^{I}(1-\\Pi\_{s}^{I})}{\\sqrt{S-s}}\\,dW\_{s}, |  |\
\
using the L2L^{2}–loss and three increasing original time costs.\
\
The left panel of Figure [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") uses\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | f⁡(s)=k⁡(s+α)1−s,c⁡(t)=k⁡(1+α−e−t)f(s)=\\frac{k(s+\\alpha)}{1-s},\\qquad c(t)=k(1+\\alpha-e^{-t}) |  |\
\
with k=0.03k=0.03 and α=1\\alpha=1. The transformed running cost is increasing, resulting in a shrinking continuation region. The middle panel uses\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | f⁡(s)=k⁡(s+α),c⁡(t)=k⁡(1+α−e−t)​e−t,f(s)=k(s+\\alpha),\\qquad c(t)=k(1+\\alpha-e^{-t})e^{-t}, |  |\
\
with k=0.24k=0.24 and α=0.01\\alpha=0.01. The transformed running cost is nonmonotone, and so the resulting optimal stopping boundary is correspondingly nonmonotone. The right panel uses the constant running cost\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | f⁡(s)=k,c⁡(t)=k​e−t,f(s)=k,\\qquad c(t)=ke^{-t}, |  |\
\
with k=0.06k=0.06. The transformed running cost is decreasing. Hence, the continuation region expands over time.\
\
These three choices show that monotone running costs in the original time scale can produce\
shrinking, nonmonotone, or expanding continuation regions after the time change.\
It can be verified that all of the examples satisfy Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). Namely, the first satisfies Assumption [B.(iii).(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i1 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and [B.(iii).(b)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i2 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") while the latter two satisfy only [B.(iii).(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i1 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
\
Figure 2. Numerical plot of the optimal stopping boundaries for the classical sequential testing win-martingale with L2L^{2}–loss and\
S=1S=1. The three panels correspond to transformed running costs that are increasing,\
nonmonotone, and decreasing, respectively. Solid curves are the optimal stopping boundaries while the dashed lines are γ\\gamma and 1−γ1-\\gamma.\
\
###### Example 8.5.\
\
Finally, we consider an oscillatory transformed running cost for the Aldous win-martingale. We take\
S=1S=1, use the cross-entropy loss, and prescribe the transformed running cost directly by\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.1) |  | c⁡(t)=k1+k2​sin⁡(2​π​t),c(t)=k\_{1}+k\_{2}\\sin(2\\pi t), |  |\
\
where k1=0.125k\_{1}=0.125 and k2=0.075k\_{2}=0.075. In the original time scale this\
corresponds to\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | f⁡(s)=k1+k2​sin⁡(−2​π​log⁡(1−s))1−s.f(s)=\\frac{k\_{1}+k\_{2}\\sin(-2\\pi\\log(1-s))}{1-s}. |  |\
\
It can easily be verified that Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") holds, specifically with Assumption [B.(iii).(b)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i2 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") by the choice of k1>k2k\_{1}>k\_{2}, while Assumption [B.(iii).(a)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I2.i3.I1.i1 "In B.(iii) ‣ Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") does not hold since gCE′g^{\\prime}\_{\\text{CE}} is unbounded.\
\
The optimal stopping boundary is plotted in Figure [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"). Since the\
transformed running cost is periodic in tt, the boundary oscillates in the transformed time\
variable. Consequently, when viewed in the original time variable ss, namely, as b⁡(A⁡(s))b(A(s)), the\
boundary need not have a limit as s↗1s\\nearrow 1. This illustrates that the local C1C^{1}–regularity\
of the boundary in transformed time cannot be extended to the terminal time point.\
We give a proof of this fact in the following proposition.\
\
Figure 3. Numerical plot of the optimal stopping boundary for the Aldous win-martingale with cross-entropy loss and\
sinusoidal transformed running cost c⁡(t)=k1+k2​sin⁡(2​π​t)c(t)=k\_{1}+k\_{2}\\sin(2\\pi t). The dashed curves show γ\\gamma and 1−γ1-\\gamma.\
\
###### Proposition 8.6.\
\
Consider an optimal stopping problem ( [2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E3 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) such that the time transformation obeys A⁡(s)→∞A(s)\\to\\infty as s→Ss\\to S. Assume, furthermore, that the transformed optimal stopping problem satisfies Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and the transformed running cost is given by ( [8.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E1 "In Example 8.5. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) for some constants k1>\|k2\|>0k\_{1}>\|k\_{2}\|>0. Then the optimal stopping boundary in the original problem s↦b∞​(A​(s))s\\mapsto b\_{\\infty}(A(s)) does not converge as s→Ss\\to S.\
\
###### Proof.\
\
Since A⁡(s)→∞A(s)\\to\\infty for s→Ss\\to S, we need to show t↦b∞​(t)t\\mapsto b\_{\\infty}(t) does not converge as t→∞t\\to\\infty. As noted in Example [8.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.Thmtheorem5 "Example 8.5. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), cc is periodic with period 11, that is, c⁡(t+1)=c⁡(t)c(t+1)=c(t). Thus t↦V⁡(t,x)t\\mapsto V(t,x) is periodic, and so by the characterizations of 𝒞\\mathcal{C} in ( [3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.E3 "In 3.1. Problem formulation ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) and ( [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.E1 "In Theorem 4.1 (Structure of the continuation region). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), it holds that t↦b∞​(t)t\\mapsto b\_{\\infty}(t) is periodic. Thus it is enough to show that the optimal stopping boundary is not constant.\
\
Assume for contradiction that b∞​(t)≡Bb\_{\\infty}(t)\\equiv B and let\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | τ⁡(x):=inf{u≥0:Xux∉(1−B,B)}.\\tau(x):=\\inf\\{u\\geq 0:X^{x}\_{u}\\not\\in(1-B,B)\\}. |  |\
\
It holds that 𝔼⁡\[τ⁡(x)\]<∞\\mathbb{E}\[\\tau(x)\]<\\infty since the diffusion is nondegenerate in (1−B,B)(1-B,B).\
Then, by ( [8.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E1 "In Example 8.5. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), the value function is given by\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.2) |  | V⁡(t,x)\\displaystyle V(t,x) | =𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτ⁡(x)x)+k1​τ​(x)\]+k2​𝔼​\[∫0τ⁡(x)sin⁡(2​π​(t+u))​𝑑u\]\\displaystyle=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau(x)})+k\_{1}\\tau(x)\\right\]+k\_{2}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\tau(x)}\\sin(2\\pi(t+u))\\,du\\right\] |  |\
| (8.3) |  |  | =H⁡(x)+k22​π​(cos⁡(2​π​t)​(1−C⁡(x))+sin⁡(2​π​t)​S​(x)),\\displaystyle=H(x)+\\frac{k\_{2}}{2\\pi}(\\cos(2\\pi t)(1-C(x))+\\sin(2\\pi t)S(x)), |  |\
\
where the second equality follows by integration and the trigonometric identity\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | cos⁡(a+b)=cos⁡(a)​cos⁡(b)−sin⁡(a)​sin⁡(b),\\cos(a+b)=\\cos(a)\\cos(b)-\\sin(a)\\sin(b), |  |\
\
and we have defined the functions\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | H⁡(x):=𝔼⁡\[g⁡(Xτ⁡(x)x)+k1​τ​(x)\],C⁡(x):=𝔼⁡\[cos⁡(2​π​τ​(x))\],S⁡(x):=𝔼⁡\[sin⁡(2​π​τ​(x))\].H(x):=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[g(X^{x}\_{\\tau(x)})+k\_{1}\\tau(x)\\right\],\\qquad C(x):=\\mathbb{E}\[\\cos(2\\pi\\tau(x))\],\\qquad S(x):=\\mathbb{E}\[\\sin(2\\pi\\tau(x))\]. |  |\
\
By Theorem [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Regularity and smooth fit). ‣ 4. Main Results ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), smooth fit implies\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | g′​(B)=H′​(B−)+k22​π​(−cos⁡(2​π​t)​C′​(B−)+sin⁡(2​π​t)​S′​(B−)),g^{\\prime}(B)=H^{\\prime}(B-)+\\frac{k\_{2}}{2\\pi}(-\\cos(2\\pi t)C^{\\prime}(B-)+\\sin(2\\pi t)S^{\\prime}(B-)), |  |\
\
for all t≥0t\\geq 0 where we have used that the terms in ( [8.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E3 "In Proof. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) are C2​((,,,))C^{2}((1-B,B)). Since this holds for all t≥0t\\geq 0, the last term must vanish. However, it is a linear combination of sine and cosine, so we must also have\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.4) |  | C′​(B−)=S′​(B−)=0.C^{\\prime}(B-)=S^{\\prime}(B-)=0. |  |\
\
Now, let u⁡(t,x):=𝔼⁡\[cos⁡(2​π​(t+τ⁡(x)))\]u(t,x):=\\mathbb{E}\[\\cos(2\\pi(t+\\tau(x)))\]. Then uu solves the Dirichlet problem,\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.5) |  | ∂tu⁡(t,x)+ℒ​u​(t,x)\\displaystyle\\partial\_{t}u(t,x)+\\mathcal{L}u(t,x) | =0,\\displaystyle=0, |  | x∈(1−B,B),\\displaystyle x\\in(1-B,B), |  |\
| (8.6) |  | u⁡(t,x)\\displaystyle u(t,x) | =cos⁡(2​π​t),\\displaystyle=\\cos(2\\pi t), |  | x∈{1−B,B},\\displaystyle x\\in\\{1-B,B\\}, |  |\
\
since the first exit time from (1−B,B)(1-B,B) is almost surely finite.\
Again\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | u⁡(t,x)=cos⁡(2​π​t)​C​(x)−sin⁡(2​π​t)​S​(x),u(t,x)=\\cos(2\\pi t)C(x)-\\sin(2\\pi t)S(x), |  |\
\
so ( [8.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E5 "In Proof. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) yields\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | cos⁡(2​π​t)​(ℒ​C​(x)−2​π​S​(x))−sin⁡(2​π​t)​(ℒ​S​(x)+2​π​C​(x))=0.\\cos(2\\pi t)\\big(\\mathcal{L}C(x)-2\\pi S(x)\\big)-\\sin(2\\pi t)\\big(\\mathcal{L}S(x)+2\\pi C(x)\\big)=0. |  |\
\
This implies\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.7) |  | ℒ​C​(x)=2​π​S​(x),ℒ​S​(x)=−2​π​C​(x),\\mathcal{L}C(x)=2\\pi S(x),\\qquad\\mathcal{L}S(x)=-2\\pi C(x), |  |\
\
for x∈(1−B,B)x\\in(1-B,B), with C⁡(B)=C⁡(1−B)=1C(B)=C(1-B)=1 and S⁡(B)=S⁡(1−B)=0S(B)=S(1-B)=0.\
\
Note that (C⁡(x),S⁡(x))(C(x),S(x)) is the mean of a unit vector. Defining\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | q⁡(x):=C2​(x)+S2​(x),q(x):=C^{2}(x)+S^{2}(x), |  |\
\
we have q⁡(x)≤1q(x)\\leq 1 and q⁡(B)=q⁡(1−B)=1q(B)=q(1-B)=1.\
Furthermore, it holds that\
\
|     |     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.8) |  | ℒ​q​(x)\\displaystyle\\mathcal{L}q(x) | =σ2​(x)​((C′​(x))2+(S′​(x))2+C⁡(x)​C′′​(x)+S⁡(x)​S′′​(x))\\displaystyle=\\sigma^{2}(x)\\left((C^{\\prime}(x))^{2}+(S^{\\prime}(x))^{2}+C(x)C^{\\prime\\prime}(x)+S(x)S^{\\prime\\prime}(x)\\right) |  |\
|  |  | =σ2​(x)​((C′​(x))2+(S′​(x))2)≥0.\\displaystyle=\\sigma^{2}(x)\\left((C^{\\prime}(x))^{2}+(S^{\\prime}(x))^{2}\\right)\\geq 0. |  |\
\
By the one-dimensional maximum principle, see e.g. \[ [29](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib29 ""), Theorem 1.1.2\], qq is either constant or q′​(B−)>0q^{\\prime}(B-)>0 since q⁡(x)≤q⁡(B)=q⁡(1−B)=1q(x)\\leq q(B)=q(1-B)=1. If q⁡(x)≡1q(x)\\equiv 1, then q′′​(x)=0q^{\\prime\\prime}(x)=0, so CC and SS are constant by ( [8.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E8 "In Proof. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), and by the boundary conditions C⁡(x)≡1C(x)\\equiv 1 and S⁡(x)≡0S(x)\\equiv 0. But then, the second expression in ( [8.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E7 "In Proof. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) states 0=−2​π0=-2\\pi, hence qq cannot be constant. Calculating q′​(B−)q^{\\prime}(B-) we get\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | 0<q′​(B−)=2​C​(B−)​C′​(B−)+2​S​(B−)​S′​(B−)=00<q^{\\prime}(B-)=2C(B-)C^{\\prime}(B-)+2S(B-)S^{\\prime}(B-)=0 |  |\
\
by ( [8.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E4 "In Proof. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")).\
Hence, we reach a contradiction to the assumption of b∞​(t)≡Bb\_{\\infty}(t)\\equiv B, proving the claim.\
∎\
\
The motivating games all exhibit the same temporal component of the diffusion ρ\\rho. However, it should be clear from the time transformation in Section [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2 "2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") that the methodology can handle more general ρ\\rho. In fact, we did not use any monotonicity relations for ρ\\rho, and similarly, we did not make use of SS being finite. We will consider a specific example arising from sequential hypothesis testing, following \[ [14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib14 "")\].\
\
###### Example 8.7.\
\
Let RR be a Gaussian random variable with mean mm and variance ξ2\\xi^{2}, and consider the observation process\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Ys=R​s+W^sY\_{s}=Rs+\\hat{W}\_{s} |  |\
\
where W^\\hat{W} is a Brownian motion in the full model.\
The goal is to test the hypothesis H0:R<0H\_{0}:R<0 against H1:R≥0H\_{1}:R\\geq 0 upon sequential observation of YY. The posterior probability process is\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | Πs=ℙ⁡(R≥0∣𝒢s)\\Pi\_{s}=\\mathbb{P}(R\\geq 0\\mid\\mathcal{G}\_{s}) |  |\
\
where (𝒢s)s≥0(\\mathcal{G}\_{s})\_{s\\geq 0} is the filtration generated by YY, and it can be shown, see \[ [14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib14 "")\], that Πs\\Pi\_{s} solves the stochastic differential equation\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.9) |  | d​Πs=ξ​φ​(Φ−1​(Πs))1+s​ξ2​d​Ws,d\\Pi\_{s}=\\frac{\\xi\\varphi(\\Phi^{-1}(\\Pi\_{s}))}{\\sqrt{1+s\\xi^{2}}}dW\_{s}, |  |\
\
with some initial condition, where WW is a Brownian motion adapted to the observation filtration (𝒢s)s≥0(\\mathcal{G}\_{s})\_{s\\geq 0}. Thus this process falls into the scope of win-martingales with separable diffusion coefficients and\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | σ⁡(π)=φ⁡(Φ−1​(π)),ρ⁡(s)=ξ1+s​ξ2\\sigma(\\pi)=\\varphi(\\Phi^{-1}(\\pi)),\\qquad\\rho(s)=\\frac{\\xi}{\\sqrt{1+s\\xi^{2}}} |  |\
\
yielding the time transformation\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | A⁡(s)=log⁡(1+s​ξ2),Γ⁡(t)=exp⁡(t)−1ξ2,A(s)=\\log(1+s\\xi^{2}),\\qquad\\Gamma(t)=\\frac{\\exp(t)-1}{\\xi^{2}}, |  |\
\
and specifically T=∞T=\\infty if S=∞S=\\infty. A typical objective in sequential hypothesis testing is to consider\
\
|     |     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- | --- |\
| (8.10) |  | v⁡(s,π):=infν∈𝒯Π𝔼⁡\[∫0νf⁡(s+u)​𝑑u+g⁡(Πνπ)\],v(s,\\pi):=\\inf\_{\\nu\\in\\mathcal{T}^{\\Pi}}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\int\_{0}^{\\nu}f(s+u)\\,du+g(\\Pi^{\\pi}\_{\\nu})\\right\], |  |\
\
where the infimum is taken over stopping times with respect to the Ππ\\Pi^{\\pi} filtration such that the expectation is finite. Here Ππ\\Pi^{\\pi} is the flow enlargement of ( [8.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E9 "In Example 8.7. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")), and gg is the terminal penalty stemming from a loss function as in Section [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2 "2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
\
In \[ [14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib14 "")\] the authors consider problems such as ( [8.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E10 "In Example 8.7. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) for more general posterior martingales, not necessarily being on separable form but having a volatility coefficient which is decreasing in time. Furthermore, they assume a constant time cost f⁡(s)≡c0>0f(s)\\equiv c\_{0}>0 and the hard classification problem. The authors go on to study this problem in great detail to prove well-posedness and regularity of the value function. In \[ [14](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib14 ""), Proposition 4.3\] the authors prove that vv is jointly continuous on \[0,∞)×\[0,1\]\[0,\\infty)\\times\[0,1\] and remark that they expect the result to hold if s↦f⁡(s)s\\mapsto f(s) is increasing. Their proof of joint continuity relies on temporal monotonicity of the value function vv, and so by similar arguments as in Proposition [8.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 8.2. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") one may indeed expect their proof to carry over.\
\
However, applying the time transformation to arrive at ( [2.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S2.E9 "In 2. A class of motivating games ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) with\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | c⁡(t)=f⁡(exp⁡(t)−1ξ2)​exp⁡(t)ξ2c(t)=f\\left(\\frac{\\exp(t)-1}{\\xi^{2}}\\right)\\frac{\\exp(t)}{\\xi^{2}} |  |\
\
we can conclude that the postulated result can be extended to general ff such that cc satisfies Assumption [A.(ii)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i2 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales"), at least in the case of the posterior probability process in ( [8.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E9 "In Example 8.7. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) stemming from the Gaussian prior, under a smooth terminal penalty, such as the one induced by L2L^{2}–loss.\
\
###### Example 8.8.\
\
If f⁡(s)≡c0>0f(s)\\equiv c\_{0}>0 in Example [8.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.Thmtheorem7 "Example 8.7. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") does not hold for large tt, making instantaneous stopping optimal for such times. Consider the L2L^{2}–loss, which gives a terminal cost of g⁡(x)=x⁡(1−x)g(x)=x(1-x). To obey Assumption [A.(v)](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.I1.i5 "In Assumption A (Baseline assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") we may consider the problem for an initial time horizon S<S′S<S^{\\prime} where\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | S′=(12​π​c0−1ξ2)+.S^{\\prime}=\\left(\\frac{1}{2\\pi c\_{0}}-\\frac{1}{\\xi^{2}}\\right)^{+}. |  |\
\
If s≥S′s\\geq S^{\\prime}, instantaneous stopping is optimal in ( [8.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E10 "In Example 8.7. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Preliminary Analysis ‣ 3. Setup and Preliminary Analysis ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales").\
If S′>0S^{\\prime}>0,\
then 0<T<T′:=log⁡(ξ2/(2​π​c0))0<T<T^{\\prime}:=\\log(\\xi^{2}/(2\\pi c\_{0})). For every such horizon TT, we note that Assumption [B](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S3.Thmtheorem0.Thmassumptionalt2 "Assumption B (Extended assumptions). ‣ 3.2. Assumptions ‣ 3. \
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | cT​(t)={c⁡(t)t≤TMT−(MT−c⁡(T))​eλT​(T−t)t>T,c\_{T}(t)=\\begin{cases}c(t)&t\\leq T\\\<br>M\_{T}-(M\_{T}-c(T))e^{\\lambda\_{T}(T-t)}&t>T,\\end{cases} |  |\
\
where MT=(1/(2​π)+c⁡(T))/2M\_{T}=(1/(2\\pi)+c(T))/2 and λT=c⁡(T)/(MT−c⁡(T))\\lambda\_{T}=c(T)/(M\_{T}-c(T)).\
For every T<T′T<T^{\\prime}, it holds that cT​(t)≥c0​ξ−2=:c¯c\_{T}(t)\\geq c\_{0}\\xi^{-2}=:\\underline{c} for all t≥0t\\geq 0. Hence the optimal stopping boundary t↦bT​(t)t\\mapsto b\_{T}(t) is decreasing by Proposition [8.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 8.2. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and bT​(t)≤B∗<1b\_{T}(t)\\leq B^{\*}<1 where B∗B^{\*} is given in Theorem [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 5.2. ‣ 5. It is thus not hard to show by monotone limit arguments, for T→T′T\\to T^{\\prime}, similar to Proposition [6.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 6.1. ‣ 6.1. Properties of the value function ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") and the end of Proposition [6.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S6.Thmtheorem13 "Proposition 6.13. ‣ 6.2. Properties of the optimal stopping boundary ‣ 6. Inhomogeneous Problems ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales") that for the infinite-horizon optimal stopping problem ( [8.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#S8.E10 "In Example 8.7. ‣ 8.2. Nonconstant optimal stopping boundaries ‣ 8. Examples ‣ When should one stop the most exciting game?Sequential Inference for win-martingales")) the optimal stopping time is given by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ν∗=inf{s∈\[0,S′):Πsπ∉(1−bT′​(A⁡(s)),bT′​(A⁡(s)))}∧S′\\nu^{\*}=\\inf\\{s\\in\[0,S^{\\prime}):\\Pi^{\\pi}\_{s}\\not\\in(1-b\_{T^{\\prime}}(A(s)),b\_{T^{\\prime}}(A(s)))\\}\\wedge S^{\\prime} |  |\
\
where t↦bT′​(t):=limT→T′bT​(t)t\\mapsto b\_{T^{\\prime}}(t):=\\lim\_{T\\to T^{\\prime}}b\_{T}(t) is decreasing with bT′​(A⁡(s))∈\[γ⁡(A⁡(s)),B∗\]b\_{T^{\\prime}}(A(s))\\in\[\\gamma(A(s)),B^{\*}\] for s<S′s<S^{\\prime} and\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | γ⁡(t):=Φ⁡(φ−1​(c0ξ​et/2)),\\gamma(t):=\\Phi\\left(\\varphi^{-1}\\left(\\frac{\\sqrt{c\_{0}}}{\\xi}e^{t/2}\\right)\\right), |  |\
\
where φ−1\\varphi^{-1} is the inverse of φ\\varphi restricted to \[0,∞)\[0,\\infty).\
\
To compare, consider the following sequential inference problem studied in \[ [13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib13 "")\],\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | v^:=infν𝔼⁡\[ℓL2​(R,R^ν)+c0​ν\],\\hat{v}:=\\inf\_{\\nu}\\mathbb{E}\\left\[\\ell\_{L^{2}}(R,\\hat{R}\_{\\nu})+c\_{0}\\nu\\right\], |  |\
\
where R^s=𝔼⁡\[R∣𝒢s\]\\hat{R}\_{s}=\\mathbb{E}\\left\[R\\mid\\mathcal{G}\_{s}\\right\]. In \[ [13](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib13 "")\] the authors show that this is a particularly simple problem, yielding a deterministic optimal stopping time given by\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ν^∗=(1c0−1ξ2)+.\\hat{\\nu}^{\*}=\\left(\\frac{1}{\\sqrt{c\_{0}}}-\\frac{1}{\\xi^{2}}\\right)^{+}. |  |\
\
That is, when seeking a point estimate of the realization of RR under an L2L^{2}–loss with a constant time cost, the optimal stopping rule is to use the point estimate at a specific deterministic time point. On the other hand, when estimating the sign of RR under an L2L^{2}–loss with a constant time cost, the optimal stopping rule is nontrivial but occurs before a different specific deterministic time point S′S^{\\prime}. Notably, there is no general order in the deterministic cutoffs of the decision times for the two problems, meaning that if, say, c0<1/(4​π2)c\_{0}<1/(4\\pi^{2}), then S′>ν^∗S^{\\prime}>\\hat{\\nu}^{\*}. However, we may of course still get ν∗<ν^∗\\nu^{\*}<\\hat{\\nu}^{\*}.\
\
## 9\. Conclusion\
\
This paper studied when to stop observing a posterior win-martingale and make a terminal decision. Starting from a sequential “stop-and-declare” problem, we reduced the Bayes risk to an optimal stopping problem for the posterior belief. For win-martingales with separable volatility, a deterministic time change removes the deterministic time factor from the dynamics and transfers it to the running cost. This gives a common framework for a general class of win-martingales such as the Aldous, Bass, and binary sequential-inference martingales, while allowing general running costs and terminal penalties.\
\
Under our standing assumptions, we characterized the optimal stopping rule by a free boundary. In the homogeneous infinite-horizon problem, the continuation region is an interval with two constant boundaries. Under the extended assumptions, the inhomogeneous finite- and infinite-horizon problems have continuation regions with time slices of the form (1−bT​(t),bT​(t))(1-b\_{T}(t),b\_{T}(t)), and the first exit from this region is optimal. We established that the value function is C1C^{1} globally and C1,2C^{1,2} away from the free boundary, proved C1C^{1} regularity of bTb\_{T} before the terminal horizon, and derived a nonlinear integral equation that characterizes the boundary uniquely. Importantly, these do not rely on monotone properties of the temporal cost.\
\
Although the analysis captures many settings, it deliberately assumes that the time-changed win-martingale remains in (0,1)(0,1) at every finite time, and therefore excludes models that can reach {0,1}\\{0,1\\} in finite time after the time change. Thus, in the present setting, the game is revealed only in the limit, or through a singular deterministic time factor at the terminal time. This leaves open the early-termination formulation considered by Guo, Howison, Possamaï, and Reisinger \[ [20](https://arxiv.org/html/2608.12291v1#bib.bib20 "")\], where the win-probability martingale may be absorbed at 00 or 11 strictly before the prescribed horizon. This includes cases such as the Wright–Fisher martingale and a Brownian motion absorbed at {0,1}\\{0,1\\}. In such models, there are two distinct stopping mechanisms. Namely, these are the decision maker’s voluntary stopping time and the exogenous termination time\
\
|     |     |     |\
| --- | --- | --- |\
|  | ζ:=inf{s≥0:Πs∈{0,1}}.\\zeta:=\\inf\\{s\\geq 0:\\Pi\_{s}\\in\\{0,1\\}\\}. |  |\
\
Extending the present theory to this setting would require incorporating absorbing endpoints that are reached in finite time and understanding how the interaction between voluntary stopping and exogenous game termination changes the geometry and regularity of the optimal boundary. We leave this case for future work.\
\
